# TABLE OF CONTENTS.

---

| | | PAGES. |
|----------|----------|----------|
| Introduction | ... ... ... ... ... | i--v |
| Chapter I—on Postures <note>original: "Asanas." These are the physical positions used in yoga to steady the body and mind.</note> | ... ... ... ... ... | 1--12 |
| " II " Breath Control <note>original: "Prânayama." This refers to the regulation of breath and the movement of vital energy or "prana."</note> | ... ... ... ... ... | 13--27 |

<term>Postures (Asanas)</term>: Physical poses intended to create stability and health in the practitioner.
<term>Breath Control (Prânayama)</term>: Rhythmic breathing exercises used to purify the nervous system and calm the mind.
<term>Seals (Mudras)</term>: Advanced physical and energetic techniques that "seal" or channel the body's vital forces.
<term>Deep Meditation (Samâdhi)</term>: The ultimate goal of yoga, where the individual consciousness merges with the universal.
<term>Yoga</term>: A system of discipline intended to lead to spiritual liberation and physical mastery.

<summary>This table of contents outlines the four-part structure of the manual, detailing the progression from physical postures to the attainment of deep meditative absorption. It serves as a roadmap for the practitioner's journey through Hatha Yoga.</summary>

<keywords>Table of Contents, Asanas, Pranayama, Mudras, Samadhi, Yoga practice stages</keywords>

<meta>This page serves as the Introduction to the 1915 edition of the Hatha Yoga Pradipika. It follows the Table of Contents from the previous page, shifting the focus from the book's structure to addressing common prejudices and misunderstandings regarding Yoga practice during the early 20th century.</meta>

# INTRODUCTION.

---

There exists at present a good deal of misconception with regard to <unclear>t</unclear>he practices of Hatha Yoga <note>A branch of yoga that emphasizes physical techniques such as postures and breath control to balance the body and mind.</note>. People easily believe in the stories told <unclear>b</unclear>y those who themselves heard them second hand, and no attempt is made to find out the truth by a direct reference to any good treatise. It <unclear>i</unclear>s generally believed that the six practices <note>original: "**षट्कर्म**" (Shatkarma). These are traditional purification techniques—such as nasal rinsing or stomach cleansing—intended to balance the body's internal systems.</note> in Hatha Yoga are compulsory on the student and that besides being dirty, they are fraught with danger to the practiser. This is not true, for these practices are necessary only in the existence of impurities in the energy channels <note>original: "Nâdis." In yogic anatomy, these are the subtle pathways through which vital energy (prana) flows.</note>, and not otherwise.

There is the same amount of misunderstanding with regard to Breath Control <note>original: "Prânâyâma." The practice of regulating the breath to stabilize the mind and life force.</note>. People put their faith implicitly in the stories told them about the dangers attending the practice, without ever taking the trouble of ascertaining the fact themselves. We have been inspiring and expiring <unclear>a</unclear>ir from our birth, and will continue to do so till death; and this <unclear>i</unclear>s done without the help of any teacher. Breath Control is nothing but a <unclear>p</unclear>roperly regulated form of the otherwise irregular and hurried flow of air, without using much force or undue restraint; and if this is accomplished <unclear>b</unclear>y patiently keeping the flow slow and steady, there can be no danger. <unclear>I</unclear>t is the impatience for supernatural powers <note>original: "Siddhis." These are spiritual or psychic perfections described in yoga literature as being attained through advanced practice.</note> which cause undue pressure on the <unclear>o</unclear>rgans and thereby causes pains in the ears, the eyes, the chest, etc. <unclear>I</unclear>f the three body locks <note>original: "**बन्ध**" (Bandhas). These are internal physical contractions—usually at the throat, abdomen, and pelvic floor—used to direct energy during breathing.</note> be carefully performed while practising Breath Control, there is no possibility of any danger.

There are two classes of students of Yoga: (1) those who study it <unclear>t</unclear>heoretically; (2) those who combine the theory with practice.

Yoga is of very little use, if studied theoretically. It was never <unclear>m</unclear>eant for such a study. In its practical form, however, the path of the <unclear>st</unclear>udent is beset with difficulties. The books on Yoga give instructions <unclear>s</unclear>o far as it is possible to express the methods in words, but all persons, <unclear>n</unclear>ot being careful enough to follow these instructions to the very letter, <unclear>fa</unclear>il in their object. Such persons require a teacher versed in the <unclear>p</unclear>ractice of Yoga. It is easy to find a teacher who will explain the language <unclear>o</unclear>f the books, but this is far from being satisfactory. For instance, a traditional scholar <note>original: "Pandit." A person with specialized knowledge of sacred texts and Sanskrit.</note> without any knowledge of the science of medicine <note>original: "Materia Medica." The branch of medical science concerned with the study of drugs and their sources.</note> will <unclear>e</unclear>xplain the word "thorny-one" <note>original: "**कंटकारि**" (Kantakari). A medicinal plant often used in Ayurvedic treatments.</note> as "an enemy of thorns," meaning shoes, <unclear>w</unclear>hile it is in reality the name of a medicinal plant.

<summary>This introduction seeks to dispel common myths about Hatha Yoga, specifically regarding the dangers of purification rituals and breath control. It emphasizes the necessity of practical experience and the guidance of an experienced teacher over mere theoretical study.</summary>

<keywords>Hatha Yoga, Shatkarma, Nâdis, Prânâyâma, Siddhis, Bandhas, Yoga Practice, Yoga Theory, Kantakari, Indian Medicine</keywords>

--- Page 5 ---
<meta>This page continues the Introduction to the 1915 edition of the Hatha Yoga Pradipika. It moves from debunking myths about physical purification to discussing the necessity of a personal teacher (Guru) and the spiritual evolution of the student across multiple lifetimes.</meta>

# INTRODUCTION.

---

The importance of a practical Yogi <note>A practitioner who has mastered the techniques of Yoga through experience rather than just theory.</note> as a guide to a student of Yoga cannot be overestimated; and without such a teacher it is next to impossible for him to achieve anything. The methods followed by the founders of the system and followed ever afterwards by their followers, have been wisely and advisedly kept secret; and this is not without a deep meaning. Looking to the gravity of the subject and the practices which have a very close relation with the vital organs of the human body, it is of paramount importance that the instructions should be received by students of ordinary capacity, through a practical teacher only, in order to avoid any possibility of mistake in practice. Speaking broadly, all men are not equally fitted to receive the instructions on equal terms. Man inherits on birth his mental and physical capitals, according to his actions in past births, and has to increase them by manipulation, but there are, even among such, different grades. Hence, one cannot become a Yogi in one incarnation <note>One single lifetime in the cycle of rebirth.</note>, as says Śri Kriṣṇa **"At the end of many births, the man of knowledge attains Me"** <note>original: "**बहूनां जन्मनामन्ते ज्ञानवान्मां प्रपद्यते**" (Bhagavad Gita 7.19). This suggests that spiritual perfection is the culmination of efforts spanning many lifetimes.</note>; and again **"Among thousands of men, one perchance strives for perfection; even among those who strive and are successful, only one, perhaps, knows Me in essence. —Gita"** <note>original: "**मनुष्याणां सहस्रेषु कश्चिद्यतति सिद्धये। यततामपि सिद्धानां कश्चिन्मां वेत्ति तत्त्वतः॥ गीता॥**" From the Bhagavad Gita (7.3), highlighting the rarity of true spiritual realization.</note>.

There are men who, impelled by the force of their actions of previous births, go headlong and accomplish their liberation in a single attempt; but others have to earn it in their successive births. If the student belongs to one of such souls and being earnest, desires from his heart to get rid of the pains of birth and death, he will find the means too. It is well-known that a true Yogi is above temptations and so to think that he keeps his knowledge secret for selling it to the highest bidder is simply absurd. Yoga is meant for the good of all creatures, and a true Yogi is always desirous of benefitting as many men as possible. But he is not to throw away this precious treasure indiscriminately. He carefully chooses its recipients, and when he finds a true and earnest student, who will not trifle with this knowledge, he never hesitates in placing his valuable treasure at the disposal of the man. What is essential in him is that he should have a real thirst for such knowledge—a thirst which will make him restless till satisfied; the thirst that will make him blind to the world and its enjoyments. He should be, in short, fired with **desire for liberation** <note>original: "**मुमुक्षुत्व**" (Mumukshutva). This is the intense longing for final spiritual emancipation from the cycle of rebirth.</note> or desire for emancipation. To such a one, there is nothing dearer than the accomplishment of this object. A true lover will risk his very life to gain union with his beloved like Tulasidâs <note>A famous 16th-century poet-saint known for his extreme devotion. The text uses him as an analogy for the intensity a student must feel for spiritual truth.</note>. A true lover will see everywhere, in every direction, in every tree and leaf, in every blade of grass his own beloved. The whole of the world, with all its beauties, is a dreary waste in his eyes, without his beloved. And he will court death, fall into

<summary>This page argues that a physical teacher (Guru) is indispensable for Yoga because the practices are powerful and potentially dangerous if misunderstood. It explains that spiritual success depends on one's efforts in previous lives and requires a singular, intense passion for liberation.</summary>
<keywords>Guru, Yoga teacher, karma, reincarnation, Bhagavad Gita, Mumukshutva, Tulasidâs, spiritual liberation, Hatha Yoga Pradipika</keywords>

# INTRODUCTION.

<unclear>the</unclear> mouth of a gaping grave, for the sake of his beloved. The student whose heart burns with such intense desire for union with the Supreme Soul <term>Paramâtmâ: The "Great Soul" or Universal Spirit</term>, <unclear>is</unclear> sure to find a teacher, and through him he will surely find Him. It is a <unclear>va</unclear>ried experience that the Supreme Soul will try to meet you half way, in proportion to the <unclear>de</unclear>gree of intensity with which you will go to meet Him. Even He <unclear>hi</unclear>mself will become your guide, direct you on to the road to success, or <unclear>pu</unclear>t you on the track to find a teacher, or lead him to you.

Well has it been said: **"Those who sought, found Him by diving into deep waters; but I, the foolish one, went to search yet remained sitting on the shore."** <note>original: "जिन ढूँढा तिन पाइयाँ गहरे पानी पैठि । मैं बावरि ढूँढन गई रही किनारे बैठि ॥" This is a well-known couplet by the mystic poet Kabir, illustrating that spiritual truths are found only by those willing to dive deep into practice.</note> It is the half-hearted who fail. They hold their <unclear>wo</unclear>rldly pleasures dearer to their hearts than their God, and therefore He <unclear>in</unclear> His turn does not consider them worthy of His favours. Says the <term>Upanisad: Ancient Sanskrit texts that form the philosophical basis of Hinduism</term>:—

> **"This Soul cannot be attained by instruction, nor by intellectual power, nor by much hearing of scriptures. Only he whom the Soul chooses can attain Him; to such a person the Soul reveals its own true form."** <note>original: "नायमात्मा प्रवचनेन लभ्यो न मेधया न बहुना श्रुतेन । यमेवैष वृणुते तेनलभ्यस्तस्यैष आत्मा विवृणुते तनूस्वाम् ॥" A famous verse from the Katha Upanisad, suggesting that divine grace is as important as personal effort.</note>

The Soul <term>âtmâ: The individual self or soul</term> will choose you as its abode only if it considers you worthy of such a favour, and not otherwise. It is therefore necessary that one should first make oneself worthy of His acceptance. Having prepared the temple (your heart) well fitted for His installation there, having cleared it of all the impurities which stink and make the place unsuitable for the highest personage to live in, and having decorated it beautifully with objects as befit that Lord of the creation, you need not wait long for Him to adorn this temple of yours which you have taken pains to make it worthy of Him. If you have done all this, He will shine in you in all His glory. In your difficult moments, when you are embarrassed, sit in a contemplative mood, and approach your Supreme Teacher <term>Parama Guru: The highest or primordial teacher, often identified with God or Ishvara</term> submissively and refer your difficulties to Him, you are sure to get the proper advice from Him. He is the Teacher of the ancients, for He is not limited by Time. <note>This is a reference to Patanjali’s Yoga Sutras (1.26): "sa pūrveṣām api guruḥ kālenānavacchedāt."</note> He instructed the ancients in bygone times, like a teacher, and if you have been unable to find a teacher in the human form, enter your <unclear>inn</unclear>er temple and consult this Great Teacher who accompanies you everywhere, and ask Him to show you the way. He knows best what is best for you. Unlike mortal beings, He is beyond the past and the future, will either send one of His agents to guide you or lead you to one and put you on the right track. He is always anxious to teach the earnest seekers, and waits for you to offer Him an opportunity to do so. But if you have not done your duty and prepared yourself worthy of entering His door, and try to gain access to His presence, laden with your unclean burden, stinking with Lust <term>Kâma</term>, Anger <term>Krodha</term>, Greed <term>Lobha</term>, and Delusion <term>Moha</term>, be sure He will keep you off from Him.

<summary>This page discusses the spiritual readiness required to find a Guru, emphasizing that the Supreme Soul (Paramâtmâ) acts as an internal guide for sincere seekers who have purified their hearts. It highlights that spiritual progress depends on the intensity of one's desire and the removal of mental impurities like lust and greed.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the introduction to the Hatha Yoga Pradipika. While the previous page focused on the spiritual longing and the role of the Supreme Soul as a guide, this page details the specific practical techniques—postures, breathing, and internal sounds—that lead the practitioner toward meditative absorption.</meta>

# INTRODUCTION.

<meta>Institutional stamps at top left and right corners. Slight foxing and staining on the right margin.</meta>

The <term>Âsanas: Yogic postures or seated positions</term> are a means of gaining steadiness of position and help to gain success in contemplation, without any distraction of the mind. If the position be not comfortable, the slightest inconvenience will draw the mind away from the <term>lakśya: The focal point or aim of meditation</term>, and so no peace of mind will be possible till the posture has ceased to cause pain by regular exercise.

Of all the various methods for concentrating the mind, repetition of <term>Praṇava: The sacred syllable 'Om'</term> or <term>Ajapâ Jâpa: The natural, spontaneous repetition of a mantra, typically the 'So-Ham' sound of the breath</term> and contemplation on its meaning is the best. It is impossible for the mind to sit idle even for a single moment, and, therefore, in order to keep it well occupied and to keep other antagonistic thoughts from entering it, repetition of the sacred 'Om' should be practised. It should be repeated till <term>Yoga Nidrâ: A state of 'yogic sleep' where the body is deeply relaxed but the consciousness remains alert</term> is induced which, when experienced, should be encouraged by slackening all the muscles of the body. <unclear>It</unclear> <note>Likely referring to the practice of Praṇava</note> will fill the mind with sacred and divine thoughts and will bring about its one-pointedness, without much effort.

<term>Anâhata Nâda: The 'unstruck' sound; internal mystical sounds heard during deep meditation</term> is awakened by the exercise of <term>Prâṇâyâma: The regulation and control of the breath</term>. A couple of weeks' practice with eighty breathing cycles in the morning and the same number in the evening will cause distinct sounds to be heard; and, as the practice will go on increasing, varied sounds become audible to the practiser. By hearing these sounds attentively one gets concentration of the mind, and thence <term>Sahaja Samâdhi: A state of natural, spontaneous absorption into the divine while remaining functional in the world</term>. When yogic sleep is experienced, the student should give himself up to it and make no efforts to check it. By and by, these sounds become subtle and they become less and less intense, so the mind loses its waywardness and becomes calm and docile; and, on this practice becoming well-established, <term>Samâdhi: The highest state of mental concentration and union with the ultimate reality</term> becomes a voluntary act. This is, however, the highest stage and is the lot of the favoured and fortunate few only.

During contemplation one sees, not with his eyes as he does the objects of the world, various colours, which the writers on Yoga call the colours of the five elements <note>The five elements (Mahabhutas) are Earth, Water, Fire, Air, and Ether, each traditionally associated with specific colors in yogic vision.</note>. Sometimes stars are seen glittering, and lightning flashes in the sky. But these are all fleeting in their nature.

At first these colours are seen in greatly agitated waves which show the unsteady condition of the mind; and as the practice increases and the mind becomes calm, these colour-waves become steady and motionless and appear as one deep ocean of light. This is the ocean in which one should dive and forget the world and become one with his Lord—which is the condition of highest bliss.

Faith in the practices of Yoga, and in one's own powers to accomplish what others have done before, is of great importance to insure speedy success. I mean "faith that will move mountains," will accomplish

<summary>This page outlines the practical steps of Hatha Yoga, moving from physical postures (Asanas) to breath control (Pranayama) and the inner hearing of mystical sounds (Anahata Nada), culminating in the visual experience of the elemental colors and the ultimate state of Samadhi.</summary>
<keywords>Asana, Pranayama, Om, Anahata Nada, Samadhi, Yoga Nidra, Five Elements, meditation practice, spiritual vision</keywords>

<meta>This page concludes the introduction to the Hatha Yoga Pradipika. Following the previous page’s discussion on the internal sounds (Anâhata Nâda), the author now emphasizes that persistent practice is the ultimate key to mastering these yogic techniques and achieving spiritual liberation.</meta>

# INTRODUCTION.

<meta>Institutional stamps at top left and right corners</meta>

anything, be it howsoever difficult. There is nothing which cannot be accomplished by practice. As Lord Śiva says in the <term>Śiva Samhitâ: An ancient Sanskrit text on Hatha Yoga, presented as a dialogue between the god Shiva and his consort Parvati</term>:

> Success is born of practice; through practice, one attains liberation. 
> 
> Perfect consciousness is gained through practice; Yoga is set in motion through practice. Success in the <term>Mudrâs: Symbolic gestures or energetic seals</term> comes from practice; from practice comes the mastery of the breath. Through practice, one cheats time, and thus becomes a conqueror of death. The power of speech and the ability to wander at will arise from the Yoga of practice. <note>original: "Abhyâsâjjâyate siddhirabhyâsânmokṣamâpnuyât. Samvidalabhate’bhyâsâ yogo’bhyâsâtpravartate. Mudrâṇāmsiddhirabhyâsâ dabhyâsâdvâyusâdhanam. Kâlavañcanamabhyâsâttathâmṛtyuñjayobhavet. Vâksiddhiḥ kâmacâritvaṃ bhavedabhyâsayogataḥ." — Chapter 4, Verses 9-11</note>

Through practice success is obtained; through practice one gains liberation.

Perfect consciousness is gained through practice; Yoga is attained through practice; success in mudrâs comes by practice. Through practice is gained success in <term>Prânâyâma: The yogic practice of breath control</term>. Death can be evaded of its prey through practice, and man becomes the conqueror of death by practice. And then let us gird up our loins, and with a firm resolution engage in the practice, having faith in the principle that **your right is to the work alone, and never to its fruits** <note>original: "karmaṇyevādhikāraste mā phaleṣu kadācana"—a famous verse from the Bhagavad Gita (2.47) emphasizing selfless action</note>, and the success must be ours. May the Almighty Father be pleased to shower His blessings on those who thus engage in the performance of their duties. Om Peace. <note>original: "Om Śam"</note>

It invokes the authority of the Shiva Samhita and the Bhagavad Gita to encourage the practitioner to begin their journey with devotion and detachment.</summary>

<meta>This page serves as the opening of the Hatha Yoga Pradipika, transitioning from the introductory philosophy of practice (Abhyâsa) found on the previous page to the formal invocation and lineage of the Hatha Yoga tradition. It establishes Hatha Yoga not as an end in itself, but as a preparatory "staircase" for the higher spiritual state of Raja Yoga.</meta>

# THE LIGHT ON HATHA YOGA.
<note>original title: "Hatha Yoga Pradipika"</note>

### CHAPTER I.
->*On Postures.*<-
<note>original: "Âsanas"</note>

->Salutation to the First Lord who taught the science of Hatha Yoga; it shines forth like a staircase for those who wish to ascend to the high pinnacled Royal Yoga. 1.<-
<note>original: "śrī-ādināthāya namo’stu tasmai yenopadiṣṭā haṭha-yoga-vidyā | vibhrājate pronnata-rāja-yogam āroḍhum icchor adhirohiṇīva || 1 ||"</note>

Salutation to <term>Âdinâtha: The "First Lord," a title for the god Shiva, regarded as the original source of yogic wisdom</term> (Siva) who expounded the knowledge of <term>Haṭha Yoga: A system of physical techniques used to prepare the body for spiritual meditation</term>, which like a staircase leads the aspirant to the high pinnacled <term>Râja Yoga: The "Royal Yoga," referring to the highest state of meditative union</term>. 1.

->Having bowed to the holy Guru and Lord, the yogi Svatmarama explains the science of Hatha solely for the sake of achieving Royal Yoga. 2.<-
<note>original: "praṇamya śrī-guruṃ nāthaṃ svātmārāmeṇa yoginā | kevalaṃ rāja-yogāya haṭha-vidyopadiśyate || 2 ||"</note>

Yogin Swâtmârâma, after saluting first his <term>Gurû: A spiritual teacher or guide</term> Srinâtha explains Haṭha Yoga for the attainment of Râja Yoga. 2.

->For those who are confused by the darkness of many conflicting opinions and do not know the Royal Yoga, the compassionate Svatmarama offers this "Light on Hatha Yoga." 3.<-
<note>original: "bhrāntyā bahumata-dhvānte rāja-yogam ajānatām | haṭha-pradīpikāṃ dhatte svātmārāmaḥ kṛpākaraḥ || 3 ||"</note>

Owing to the darkness arising from the multiplicity of opinions people are unable to know the Râja Yoga. Compassionate Swâtmârâma composes the Haṭha Yoga Pradipikâ like a torch to dispel it. 3.

->The science of Hatha is known by masters such as Matsyendra and Goraksha; by their grace, the yogi Svatmarama also knows it. 4.<-
<note>original: "haṭha-vidyāṃ hi matsyendra-gorakṣādyā vijānate | svātmārāmo’thavā yogī jānīte tat-prasādataḥ || 4 ||"</note>

Matsyendra, Gorakṣa, etc., knew Haṭha Vidyâ, and by their favour Yogi Swâtmârâma also learnt it from them. 4.

->The First Lord (Shiva), Matsyendra, Shabara, Anandabhairava, Chaurangi, Meena, Goraksha, Virupaksha, and Bileshaya. 5.<-
<note>original: "śrī-ādinātha-matsyendra-śāvarānanda-bhairavāḥ | cau-raṅgī-mīna-gorakṣa-virūpākṣa-bileśayāḥ || 5 ||"</note>

The following <term>Siddhas: "Perfected ones" or masters who have attained spiritual and physical liberation</term> are said to have existed in former times:—
Sri Âdinâtha (Siva), Matsyendra, Nâtha, Śabar, Anand, Bhairava, Chaurangi, Mina nâtha, Gorakṣanâtha, Virupâkṣa, Bileśaya. 5.

->Manthana, Bhairava, Siddhi, Buddha, Kanthadi, Korantaka, Surananda, Siddhipada, and Charpati. 6.<-
<note>original: "manthāno bhairavo yogī siddhir buddhaś ca kaṃthaḍiḥ | koraṃṭakaḥ surānandaḥ siddhipādaś ca carpaṭiḥ || 6 ||"</note>

Manthâna, Bhairava, Siddhi Buddha, Kanthadi, Karantaka, Surânanda, Siddhipâda, Charapati. 6.

<summary>This page begins the first chapter of the Hatha Yoga Pradipika, featuring an invocation to the god Shiva (Adinatha) and a lineage of legendary masters (Siddhas). The author, Svatmarama, clarifies that the physical practices of Hatha Yoga are intended as a preparatory stage to reach the ultimate spiritual state of Raja Yoga.</summary>
<keywords>Hatha Yoga, Raja Yoga, Adinatha, Svatmarama, Matsyendra, Goraksha, Siddhas, Asanas, lineage, spiritual enlightenment</keywords>

--- Page 10 ---
<meta>This page continues the lineage of the Great Masters (Mahasiddhas) of the Hatha Yoga tradition and transitions into practical instructions for the practitioner, specifically regarding the secrecy of practice and the physical environment required for success.</meta>

# THE LIGHT ON HATHA YOGA.
<note>original title: "Hatha Yoga Pradipika"</note>

->Kânerî, Pûjyapâda, Nityanâtha, Nirañjana, Kapâli, Vindunâtha, and Kâka Chandîśwara. 7.<-

->Allâma, Prabhudeva, Ghodâ, Cholî, Tintini, Bhânukî, Nârdeva, and Khanda Kâpâlika, etc. 8.<-

->These <term>Mahâsiddhas: "Great Adepts" or masters who have attained spiritual perfection and supernatural powers</term>, having broken the staff of death <note>original: "khaṇḍayitvā kāladaṇḍaṃ" — signifying they have overcome the cycle of mortality and time</note>, roam throughout the universe. 9.<-

->For those scorched by the endless heat of suffering, Haṭha Yoga is a sheltering hermitage. For those devoted to any form of yoga, Haṭha is the supporting tortoise <note>In Hindu cosmology, the world is supported on the back of a great tortoise; here, Hatha Yoga is described as the essential foundation for all other spiritual practices</note>. 10.<-
<note>The "endless heat" refers to the "Three Tapas" (Tritapa): the threefold miseries of life caused by oneself, other living beings, and natural or divine forces.</note>

->A <term>Yogî: A practitioner of yoga</term> who desires <term>Siddhi: Success, perfection, or the attainment of spiritual powers</term> should keep the science of Haṭha Yoga strictly secret. It is potent when kept hidden, but loses its power when revealed to the world. 11.<-

->The Yogî should practice Haṭha Yoga in a small room, situated in a solitary place, roughly the size of a bow’s length <note>approx. 6 feet or 4 cubits</note> in each direction. It should be free from stones, fire, water, and disturbances of all kinds. This should be in a country where justice is properly administered, where the people are virtuous, and where food can be obtained easily and in abundance. 12.<-

->The masters of Haṭha practice have described the ideal characteristics of a yoga hermitage as follows: it should have a small door and no holes, cracks, or crevices. It should be neither too high nor too low. It must be clean, smeared well with cow dung <note>Traditionally used in ancient India for its antiseptic and purifying qualities</note>, and entirely free from insects. On the outside, there should be a small hall, a raised platform, and a well, with the entire area surrounded by a wall. 13.<-

<summary>This page lists the legendary masters of the Hatha Yoga lineage and provides specific instructions for the practitioner's environment, emphasizing secrecy and the construction of a simple, clean hermitage.</summary>
<keywords>Mahasiddhas, lineage, Hatha Yoga, hermitage, secrecy, Siddhi, yoga practice environment</keywords>

--- Page 11 ---
<meta>This page continues the specific instructions for a Hatha Yoga practitioner's environment and introduces the ethical foundations of the practice. It transitions from the physical requirements of the practice room to the mental and behavioral qualities that either hinder or support the student’s progress, before beginning the discussion on physical postures.</meta>

# THE LIGHT ON HATHA YOGA.
<note>original title: "Hatha Yoga Pradipika"</note>

The room should have a small door, be free from holes or hollows, and be neither too high nor too low. It should be well plastered with cow-dung <note>In traditional Indian architecture, cow-dung was used as a natural, antiseptic plaster for floors and walls to keep the space clean and cool</note> and free from dirt, filth, and insects. On its outside, there should be bowers, a <term>chabootrâ: a raised stone or earthen platform used for sitting or meditation</term>, a well, and a surrounding wall. These characteristics of a room for Haṭha Yogîs have been described by those who have reached perfection in the practice. 13.

->Remaining in such a hermitage, free from all anxieties,<-
->one should practice Yoga alone, following the path taught by the <term>guru: a spiritual teacher or guide</term>. 14.<-
<note>original Sanskrit: "evaṃvidhe maṭhe sthitvā sarvacintāvivarjitaḥ | gurūpadiṣṭamārgeṇa yogameva samabhyaset ||"</note>

Being seated in such a room and free from all anxieties, he should practise Yoga, as instructed by his teacher. 14.

->Over-eating, over-exertion, talkativeness, and adherence to rigid rules—<-
->such as cold baths in the morning, eating only at night, or eating only fruit—<-
->as well as seeking the company of common people and unsteadiness:<-
->by these six causes, Yoga is destroyed. 15.<-
<note>original Sanskrit: "atyāhāraḥ prayāsaśca prajalpo niyamāgrahaḥ | janasaṅgaśca laulyaṃ ca ṣaḍbhiryogo vinaśyati ||"</note>

Yoga is destroyed by the following six causes:—Over-eating, exertion, talkativeness, adhering to rules, *i.e.*, cold bath in the morning, eating at night, or eating fruits only, company of men, and unsteadiness. 15.

->Enthusiasm, daring, perseverance, discriminative knowledge,<-
->firm determination, and abandoning the company of common people:<-
->by these six, Yoga is successfully achieved. 16.<-
<note>original Sanskrit: "utsāhātsāhasāddhairyāttattvajñānācca niścayāt | janasaṅgaparityāgātṣaḍbhiryogaḥ prasiddhyati ||"</note>

The following six bring speedy success:—Courage, daring, perseverance, discriminative knowledge, faith, aloofness from company. 16.

->**Now, the Restraints and Observances.**<-

->Non-violence, truth, non-stealing, continence, forgiveness, fortitude,<-
->compassion, honesty, a moderate diet, and cleanliness: these are the ten <term>yamas: ethical restraints or moral rules of conduct</term>. 17.<-
<note>original Sanskrit: "ahiṃsā satyamasteyaṃ brahmacaryaṃ kṣamā dhṛtiḥ | dayārjavaṃ mitāhāraḥ śaucaṃ caiva yamā daśa ||"</note>

The ten rules of conduct are: <term>ahimsâ</term> (non-injuring), truth, non-stealing, continence, forgiveness, endurance, compassion, meekness, sparing diet and cleanliness. 17.

->Austerity, contentment, faith, charity, worship of the Divine,<-
->listening to the explanation of spiritual doctrines, modesty, discernment,<-
->prayer, and sacrifice: these are the ten <term>niyamas: internal observances or virtuous habits</term>,<-
->as declared by those experts in the science of Yoga. 18.<-
<note>original Sanskrit: "tapaḥ saṃtoṣa āstikyaṃ dānamīśvarapūjanam | siddhāntavākyaśravaṇaṃ hrīmati ca tapo hutam | niyamā daśa saṃproktā yogaśāstraviśāradaiḥ ||"</note>

The ten observances mentioned by those proficient in the knowledge of yoga are: <term>Tapa: austerity or self-discipline</term>, patience, belief in God, charity, adoration of God, hearing discourses on the principles of religion, shame, intellect, recitation of prayers and <term>Yajña: ritual sacrifice or offering</term>. 18.

->**Now, the Postures.**<-

->As it is the first limb of Haṭha Yoga, posture is described first.<-
->The practice of posture brings stability, health, and lightness of the body. 19.<-
<note>original Sanskrit: "haṭhasya prathamāṅgatvādāsanaṃ pūrvamucyate | kuryāttadāsanaṃ sthairyamārogyaṃ cāṅgalāghavam ||"</note>

<meta>This page transitions from the general requirements of the practitioner and their environment to the specific physical practices of Hatha Yoga. It begins the systematic description of postures (āsanas), which are the foundational physical exercises of the system. This page introduces the first five specific postures.</meta>

# THE LIGHT ON HATHA YOGA.
<note>original title: "Hatha Yoga Pradipika"</note>

### Postures.
<note>original: "Âsanas"</note>

Being the first accessory of <term>Hatha Yoga: a discipline of physical techniques designed to balance the body's energies</term>, posture is described first. It should be practiced for gaining steady posture, health, and lightness of body. 19.

<note>original Sanskrit: "vasiṣṭhādyaiśca munibhir matsyendrādyaiśca yogibhiḥ || aṅgīkṛtānyāsanāni kathyante kānicinmayā ||"</note>
I am going to describe certain <term>āsanas: physical postures</term> which have been adopted by <term>munis: ancient sages</term> like Vasiṣṭha and others, and by <term>yogis: practitioners of yoga</term> like Matsyendra and others. 20.

<note>original Sanskrit: "jānvorantare samyak kṛtvā pādātale ubhe || ṛjukāyaḥ samāsīnaḥ svastikaṃ tatpracakṣate ||"</note>
->The Auspicious Posture<-
<note>original: "Swastika-âsana"</note>

Having placed both the soles of the feet properly between the knees and the thighs, with the body held straight, when one sits calmly in this position, it is called the Auspicious Posture. 21. <note>The transcription mentions "hands," but the Sanskrit "pādātale" refers to the soles of the feet being tucked into the leg joints.</note>

<note>original Sanskrit: "savye dakṣiṇagulphaṃ tu pṛṣṭhapārśve niyojayet || dakṣiṇe'pi tathā savyaṃ gomukhaṃ gomukhākṛti ||"</note>
->The Cow-Face Posture<-
<note>original: "Gomukha-âsana"</note>

Placing the right ankle on the left side of the seat and the left ankle on the right side, one performs the Cow-Face Posture, which takes the appearance of a cow's head. 22.

<note>original Sanskrit: "ekaṃ pādaṃ tathaikasmin vinyasedūruṇi sthitam || itarasmiṃstathā coruṃ vīrāsanamitīritam ||"</note>
->The Hero Posture<-
<note>original: "Vîrâsana"</note>

One foot is to be placed on the thigh of the opposite side, and the other foot likewise on the other opposite thigh. This is declared to be the Hero Posture. 23.

<note>original Sanskrit: "gudaṃ nirudhya gulphābhyāṃ vyutkrameṇa samāhitaḥ || kūrmāsanaṃ bhavedetaditi yogavido viduḥ ||"</note>
->The Tortoise Posture<-
<note>original: "Kurmâsana"</note>

By crossing the ankles in reverse and firmly pressing against the <term>anus: the base of the torso</term> while remaining composed, one performs what the masters of Yoga know as the Tortoise Posture. 24.

<note>original Sanskrit: "padmāsanaṃ tu saṃsthāpya jānvorantare karau || niveśya bhūmau saṃsthāpya vyomasthaṃ kukkuṭāsanam ||"</note>
->The Rooster Posture<-
<note>original: "Kukkuta âsana"</note>

First taking the <term>Lotus Posture: a seated position with feet crossed on opposite thighs</term> and then inserting the hands between the thighs and the knees, the Yogi rests his palms on the ground and lifts himself into the air; this becomes the Rooster Posture. 25.

<summary>This page introduces the physical practice of Hatha Yoga, defining the purpose of postures (āsanas) as health and stability, and providing instructions for five specific positions: Swastika, Gomukha, Vira, Kurma, and Kukkuta.</summary>
<keywords>Hatha Yoga, Asana, Swastikasana, Gomukhasana, Virasana, Kurmasana, Kukkutasana, Vasiṣṭha, Matsyendra, health, physical stability</keywords>

--- Page 13 ---
<meta>This page continues the description of specific hatha yoga postures (āsanas), following the Swastika and Gomukha postures from the previous page. It details the physical execution and the spiritual/medicinal benefits of four additional postures: the Extended Tortoise, the Bow, the Fish-Lord, and the Back-Stretch.</meta>

# THE LIGHT ON HATHA YOGA.
<note>original title: "The Hatha Yoga Pradipika"</note>

<note>original Sanskrit: "kukkuṭāsanabandhastho dorbhyāṃ saṃbadhya kandharām || bhavetkūrmavaduttāna etaduttānakūrmakam || 26 ||"</note>
->Established in the Cock Posture, binding the neck with the two arms, one should lie on the back like a tortoise; this is the Extended Tortoise Posture. 26.<-

->**The Extended Tortoise Posture**<-
<note>original: "Uttâna Kûrma-âsana"</note>

Having assumed <term>Kukkuṭa-âsana: the Cock Posture, where the arms are pinned through the legs</term>, when one grasps his neck by crossing his hands behind his head, and lies in this posture with his back touching the ground, it becomes the Extended Tortoise Posture, named for its appearance like that of a tortoise. 26.

<note>original Sanskrit: "pādāṅguṣṭhau tu pāṇibhyāṃ gṛhītvā śravaṇāvadhi || dhanurākarṣaṃ kuryāddhanurasanamuchyate || 27 ||"</note>
->Having caught the big toes with the hands and drawn them to the ears as if pulling a bowstring, this is called the Bow Posture. 27.<-

->**The Bow Posture**<-
<note>original: "Dhanura âsana"</note>

Having caught the toes of the feet with both the hands and carried them to the ears by drawing the body like a bow, it becomes the Bow Posture. 27.

<note>original Sanskrit: "vāmorumūlārpita-dakṣapādaṃ jānorbahirveṣṭita-vāmapādam || pragṛhya tiṣṭhet parivartitāṅgaḥ śrīmatsyanāthoditamāsanaṃ syāt || 28 || matsyendrapīṭhaṃ jaṭharapradīptiṃ pracaṇḍarugmaṇḍalakhaṇḍanāstram || abhyāsataḥ kuṇḍalinīprabodhaṃ candrasthiratvaṃ ca dadāti puṃsām || 29 ||"</note>
->Place the right foot at the base of the left thigh, and wrap the left foot around the outside of the knee. Grasping these and remaining with the body twisted, this is the posture described by the venerable Lord Matsyendra. 28.<-
->The Seat of Matsyendra kindles the digestive fire and is a weapon that shatters the array of terrible diseases. With practice, it awakens the <term>Kundalinî: the dormant spiritual energy at the base of the spine</term> and grants stability to the "moon" within men. 29.<-

->**The Posture of Matsyendra**<-
<note>original: "Matsya-âsana." Note: While the text uses "Matsya-âsana," it refers to the seated twist now commonly known as Matsyendrāsana, named after the sage Matsyendranātha.</note>

Having placed the right foot at the root of the left thigh, let the toe be grasped with the right hand passing over the back, and having placed the left foot on the right thigh at its root, let it be grasped with the left hand passing behind the back. This is the posture, as explained by the venerable <term>Matsyanâtha: an ancient founding master of Hatha Yoga</term>. It increases appetite and is an instrument for destroying the group of the most deadly diseases. Its practice awakens the Kundalinî and stops the nectar shedding from the "moon" <note>the lunar center in the head which is said to leak vital energy downward</note> in people. 28-29.

<note>original Sanskrit: "prasārya pādau bhuvi daṇḍarūpau dorbhyāṃ padāgradvitayaṃ gṛhītvā || jānūpari nyastalalāṭadeśo vasedidam paścimatānamāhuḥ || 30 ||"</note>
->Stretching both legs on the ground like a staff and grasping the tips of the feet with the hands, one should rest the forehead upon the knees; this they call the Back-Stretch Posture. 30.<-

->**The Back-Stretching Posture**<-
<note>original: "Paschima Tâna," now commonly called Paschimottānāsana.</note>

Having stretched the feet on the ground, like a stick, and having grasped the toes of both the feet with both the hands, when one sits with his forehead resting on the thighs, it is called the Back-Stretch Posture. 30.

<note>original Sanskrit: "iti paścimatānamāsanāgryaṃ pavanaṃ paścimavāhinaṃ karoti || udayaṃ jaṭharānalasya kuryādudare kārśyamarogatāṃ ca puṃsām || 31 ||"</note>
->This Back-Stretch is the foremost of postures; it makes the breath flow through the posterior channel, kindles the digestive fire, makes the belly lean, and bestows health upon men. 31.<-

This Back-Stretch carries the air from the front to the back part of the body (that is, to the <term>suṣumṇâ: the central energy channel of the spine</term>). It kindles gastric fire, reduces obesity, and cures all diseases of men. 31.

<summary>This page describes four major yoga postures—Uttānakūrmāsana, Dhanurāsana, Matsyendrāsana, and Paschimottānāsana—outlining their physical alignment and their roles in awakening spiritual energy (Kundalini) and improving digestion.</summary>
<keywords>Uttānakūrmāsana, Dhanurāsana, Matsyendrāsana, Paschimottānāsana, Kundalini, Suṣumṇā, digestive fire, Matsyendranātha, Hatha Yoga</keywords>

--- Page 14 ---
<meta>This page continues the catalog of physical postures (āsanas), following the description of the Matsyendra posture. It details the execution and medicinal benefits of the Peacock and Corpse postures, transitions into the four primary postures favored by Lord Shiva, and provides the technique for the Adept’s Posture.</meta>

# THE LIGHT ON HATHA YOGA
<note>original title: "The Hatha Yoga Pradipika"</note>

<note>original Sanskrit: "dharāmavaṣṭabhya karadvayena tatkūrparasthāpitanābhipārśvaḥ || uccāsano daṇḍavadutthitaḥ syāmmayūrametatpravadanti pīṭham || 32 ||"</note>
->Supporting the body on the ground with the palms of both hands, placing the navel upon both elbows, and balancing thus, the body should be stretched backward and held straight like a stick. This is called the Peacock Posture. 32.<-

->***The Peacock Posture***<-
<note>original: "Mayūra-âsana"</note>

Place the palms of both hands on the ground, and place the navel on both the elbows; balancing thus, the body should be stretched backward like a stick. This is called the Peacock Posture. 32.

<note>original Sanskrit: "haratisakalarogānāśu gulmodarādīnabhibhavati ca doṣānāsanaṃ śrīmayūram || bahu kadaśanabhuktaṃ bhasma kuryādaśeṣaṃ janayati jaṭharāgniṃ jārayetkālakūṭam || 33 ||"</note>
->The glorious Peacock Posture quickly destroys all diseases, such as enlargement of the glands and abdominal disorders, and overcomes imbalances in the humors. It completely digests unwholesome food taken in excess, kindles the digestive fire, and even neutralizes the most deadly poison. 33.<-

This posture soon destroys all diseases, and removes abdominal disorders, and also those arising from irregularities of <term>the humors: the three biological energies known as phlegm (kapha), bile (pitta), and wind (vāta)</term>, digests unwholesome food taken in excess, increases appetite and destroys the most deadly poison. 33.

<note>original Sanskrit: "uttānaṃ śavavadbhūmau śayanaṃ tacchavāsanam || śavāsanaṃ śrāntiharaṃ cittaviśrāntikārakam || 34 ||"</note>
->Lying flat on the ground on one's back like a corpse is called the Corpse Posture. The Corpse Posture removes fatigue and grants rest to the mind. 34.<-

->***The Corpse Posture***<-
<note>original: "Śava-âsana"</note>

Lying down on the ground, like a corpse, is called the Corpse Posture. It removes fatigue and gives rest to the mind. 34.

<note>original Sanskrit: "caturaśītyāsanāni śivena kathitāni ca || tebhyaścatuṣkamādāya sārabhūtaṃ bravīmyaham || 35 ||"</note>
->Eighty-four postures were taught by Lord Shiva. Taking the four essential ones from among them, I shall now describe them. 35.<-

<term>Lord Shiva: traditionally regarded as the first teacher of Hatha Yoga</term> taught 84 postures. Of these the first four being essential ones, I am going to explain them here. 35.

<note>original Sanskrit: "siddhaṃ padmaṃ tathā siṃhaṃ bhadraṃ ceti catuṣṭayam || śreṣṭhaṃ tatrāpi ca sukhe tiṣṭhetsiddhāsane sadā || 36 ||"</note>
->These four are the Adept, the Lotus, the Lion, and the Gracious. Even among these, the Adept’s Posture is the best; one should always practice it for comfort. 36.<-

These four are:—The Adept (*Siddha*), the Lotus (*Padma*), the Lion (*Sinha*) and the Gracious (*Bhadra*). Even of these, the Adept's Posture, being very comfortable, one should always practice it. 36.

<note>original Sanskrit: "yonisthānakamaṅghrimūlaghaṭitaṃ kṛtvā dṛḍhaṃ vinyaset meḍhre pādamathaikameva hṛdaye kṛtvāhanuṃ susthiram || sthāṇuḥ saṃyamitendriyo'caladṛśā paśyedbhruvorantaraṃ hyetanmokṣakapāṭabhedajanakaṃ siddhāsanaṃ procyate || 37 ||"</note>
->Placing the heel of one foot firmly against the perineum and the other foot placed over the male organ, with the chin fixed firmly on the chest, one should remain motionless with the senses restrained. With a steady gaze, one should look at the space between the eyebrows. This is called the Adept’s Posture, which forces open the door to liberation. 37.<-

->***The Adept’s Posture***<-
<note>original: "Siddhâsana"</note>

Press firmly the heel of the left foot against the <term>perineum: the region between the anus and the genitals</term>, and the right heel above the male organ. With the chin pressing on the chest, one should sit calmly, having restrained the senses, and gaze steadily at the space between the eyebrows. This is called the Adept’s Posture, the opener of the door of salvation. 37.

<summary>This page describes the techniques and medicinal benefits of the Peacock and Corpse postures before identifying the four most important yoga poses taught by Lord Shiva. It concludes with detailed instructions for the Adept’s Posture, highlighting its role in spiritual liberation.</summary>

<keywords>Peacock Posture, Corpse Posture, Adept’s Posture, Mayūrasana, Śavāsana, Siddhāsana, Lord Shiva, digestion, detoxification, meditation, humors, salvation</keywords>

--- Page 15 ---
<meta>This page continues the instruction on physical postures, moving from the Peacock and Corpse postures to a deep exploration of the Adept’s Posture (Siddhāsana). It establishes this posture as the most vital of all 84 techniques for purifying the body's energy network.</meta>

# THE LIGHT ON HATHA YOGA
<note>original title: "The Hatha Yoga Pradipika"</note>

->According to another tradition:<-
<note>original: "matāṃtare tu"</note>

->Place the left heel above the <term>genitals: Medhra</term>, and then place the right heel upon that. This becomes the <term>Adept’s Posture: Siddhāsana</term>. 38.<-
<note>original: "meḍhrādupari vinyasya savyaṃ gulphaṃ tathopari || gulphāntaraṃ ca nikṣipya siddhāsanamidaṃ bhavet || 38 ||"</note>

This Adept’s Posture is also performed by placing the left heel above the male organ, and then placing the right one over it. 38.

->Some call this the Adept's Posture; others know it as the <term>Thunderbolt Posture: Vajrāsana</term>. Some call it the <term>Liberated Posture: Muktāsana</term>, while others call it the <term>Hidden Posture: Guptāsana</term>. 39.<-
<note>original: "etatsiddhāsanaṃ prāhuranye vajrāsanaṃ viduḥ || muktāsanaṃ vadantyeke prāhurguptāsanaṃ pare || 39 ||"</note>

Some call this the Adept's Posture, while some call it the Thunderbolt Posture. Others refer to it as the Liberated Posture or the Hidden Posture. 39.

->Just as a moderate diet is the most important of the <term>ethical restraints: Yamas</term>, and non-violence is the most important of the <term>spiritual observances: Niyamas</term>, so the Adept’s Posture is known by the perfected ones as the chief of all postures. 40.<-
<note>original: "yameṣviva mitāhāramahiṃsāṃ niyameṣviva || mukhyaṃ sarvāsaneṣvekaṃ siddhāḥ siddhāsanaṃ viduḥ || 40 ||"</note>

Just as a sparing diet is foremost among the restraints, and non-violence among the observances, so the Adept’s Posture is regarded by the perfected ones as the most important of all the postures. 40.

->Among the eighty-four postures, one should always practice the Adept’s Posture, for it cleanses the impurities from the seventy-two thousand <term>energy channels: nādīs</term>. 41.<-
<note>original: "caturaśītipīṭheṣu siddhameva sadābhyaset || dvāsaptatisahasrāṇāṃ nāḍīnāṃ malaśodhanam || 41 ||"</note>

Out of the 84 postures, the Adept’s Posture should always be practiced, because it cleanses the impurities of the 72,000 energy channels. 41.

->By meditating on the Self, eating moderately, and practicing the Adept’s Posture for twelve years, the practitioner achieves <term>perfection: niṣpatti</term>. 42.<-
<note>original: "ātmadhyāyī mitāhārī yāvaddvādaśavatsaram || sadā siddhāsana-abhyāsādyogī niṣpattimāpnuyāt || 42 ||"</note>

By contemplating one's own nature, by eating sparingly, and by practicing the Adept’s Posture for 12 years, the Yogi obtains success. 42.

->What is the use of many other postures when the Adept’s Posture is mastered, the <term>vital breath: Prāna Vâyû</term> becomes calm, and is restrained by <term>spontaneous breath retention: Kevala Kumbhaka</term>? 43.<-
<note>original: "kimanyairbahubhiḥ pīṭhaiḥ siddhe siddhāsane sati || prāṇānile sāvadhāne baddhe kevalakumbhake || 43 ||"</note>

Other postures are of no use when success has been achieved in the Adept’s Posture, and the vital breath becomes calm and restrained by spontaneous breath retention. 43.

->When the Adept's Posture alone is firmly established, the state of <term>mindlessness: Unmanī</term> arises effortlessly, and the <term>three energetic locks: Bandhas</term> occur of their own accord. 44.<-
<note>original: "utpadyate nirāyāsātsvayamevonmanī kalā || tathaikasminneva dṛḍhe siddhe siddhāsane sati | bandhatrayamanāyāsātsvayamevopajāyate || 44 ||"</note>

When success in the Adept’s Posture alone becomes firmly established, one reaches the state of "mindlessness" at once, and the three energetic bonds are accomplished of themselves. 44.

->There is no posture like the Adept’s Posture, no breath retention like the spontaneous one, no <term>seal: Mudrā</term> like the <term>Sky-walker: Khecharī</term>, and no <term>absorption: Laya</term> like the <term>inner sound: Nāda</term>. 45.<-
<note>original: "nāsanaṃ siddhasadṛśaṃ na kumbhaḥ kevalopamaḥ || na khecarīsamā mudrā na nādasadṛśo layaḥ || 45 ||"</note>

<note>Glossary of technical terms used: Adept’s Posture (Siddhâsana), Genitals (Medhra), Thunderbolt Posture (Vajrâsana), Liberated Posture (Mukta Âsana), Hidden Posture (Gupta Âsana), Moderate Diet (Mitâhâra), Non-violence (Ahimsâ), Ethical Restraints (Yamas), Spiritual Observances (Niyamas), Energy Channels (Nâdîs), Spontaneous Breath Retention (Kevala Kumbhaka), Vital Breath (Prâna Vâyû), Mindlessness (Unmanî), Energetic Locks (Bandhas), Sky-walking Seal (Khecharî Mudrâ), Inner Sound (Nâda)</note>

<summary>This page highlights Siddhāsana (the Adept's Posture) as the most important of all Hatha Yoga postures. It claims that mastering this single pose for twelve years, combined with a moderate diet, can purify the entire nervous system and lead to spontaneous spiritual liberation.</summary>
<keywords>Siddhāsana, Adept's Posture, Nādīs, Kevala Kumbhaka, energy purification, Hatha Yoga, breath retention, Unmanī</keywords>

# THE LIGHT ON HATHA YOGA
<note>original title: "The Hatha Yoga Pradipika"</note>

There is no <term>posture: Āsana</term> like the <term>Adept’s Posture: Siddhāsana</term> and no <term>breath retention: Kumbhaka</term> like the <term>Spontaneous Retention: Kevala</term>. There is no <term>seal: Mudrā</term> like the <term>Sky-moving Seal: Khecharî</term> and no <term>absorption: Laya</term> like the <term>Internal Sound: Nâda</term> (the Unstruck Sound). 45.

### Now, the Lotus Posture
<note>original: "atha padmāsanaṃ"</note>

->Place the right foot on the left thigh and the left foot on the right thigh;<-
->crossing the hands behind the back, firmly grasp the toes.<-
->Place the chin upon the chest and gaze at the tip of the nose.<-
->This is called the <term>Lotus Posture: Padmāsana</term>, which destroys the diseases of the <term>restrained ones: Yamīs</term>. 46.<-
<note>original: "vāmorūpari dakṣiṇaṃ ca caraṇaṃ saṃsthāpya vāmaṃ tathā dakṣo rūpari paścimena vidhinā dhṛtvā karābhyāṃ dṛḍham | aṅguṣṭhau hṛdaye nidhāya cibukaṃ nāsāgramālokayet etadvyādhivināśakāri yamināṃ padmāsanaṃ procyate || 46 ||"</note>

*The Lotus Posture.*

Place the right foot on the left thigh and the left foot on the right thigh, and grasp the toes with the hands crossed over the back. Press the chin against the chest and gaze on the tip of the nose. This is called the Lotus Posture, the destroyer of the diseases of those who practice self-restraint. 46.

->Carefully place the feet on the thighs with the soles facing upward;<-
->likewise, place the hands in the lap with the palms upward. 47.<-
<note>original: "uttānau caraṇau kṛtvā ūrusaṃsthau prayatnataḥ || ūrumadhye tathottānau pāṇī kṛtvā tato dṛśau || 47 ||"</note>

Place the feet on the thighs, with the soles upwards, and place the hands on the thighs, with the palms upwards. 47.

->Fix the gaze on the tip of the nose and the tongue at the root of the upper teeth;<-
->press the chin against the chest and slowly raise the breath upward. 48.<-
<note>original: "nāsāgre vinyasedrājadantamūle tu jihvayā || uttambhya cibukaṃ vakṣasyutthāpya pavanaṃ śanaiḥ || 48 ||"</note>

Gaze on the tip of the nose, keeping the tongue pressed against the root of the teeth of the upper jaw, and the chin against the chest, and raise the air up slowly—that is, pull the <term>downward-moving energy: apâna-vâyû</term> gently upwards. 48.

->This is declared to be the Lotus Posture, the destroyer of all diseases.<-
->It is difficult for most to attain, but is mastered by the wise in this world. 49.<-
<note>original: "idaṃ padmāsanaṃ proktaṃ sarvavyādhivināśanam || durlabhaṃ yena kenāpi dhīmatā labhyate bhuvi || 49 ||"</note>

This is called the Lotus Posture, the destroyer of all diseases. It is difficult of attainment by everybody, but can be learnt by intelligent people in this world. 49.

->Having placed the hands together in the lap and established the Lotus Posture firmly,<-
->press the chin closely against the chest and meditate upon Him in the mind.<-
->Repeatedly draw the downward breath upward and push the inhaled breath downward;<-
->by the power of this <term>primordial energy: Śakti</term>, one attains incomparable awakening as the <term>vital breaths: Prāṇa</term> meet. 50.<-
<note>original: "kṛtvā saṃpuṭitau karau dṛḍhataraṃ badhvā tu padmāsanaṃ gāḍhaṃ vakṣasi sannidhāya cibukaṃ dhyāyaṃśca taccetasi | vāraṃ vāramapānamūrdhvamanilaṃ protsārayanpūritaṃ nyañcanprāṇamupaiti bodhamatulaṃ śaktiprabhāvānnaraḥ || 50 ||"</note>

Having kept both the hands together in the lap, performing the Lotus Posture firmly, keeping the chin fixed to the chest and contemplating on the Divine in the mind, by drawing the downward energy up (performing the <term>Root Lock: Mûla Bandha</term>) and pushing down the air after inhaling it, one joins the vital breaths together. Through the power of the goddess-energy, the practitioner reaches unsurpassed enlightenment. 50.

<summary>This page describes the technical execution of Padmāsana (Lotus Posture), detailing two variations: the "Bound" Lotus and the meditative version with hands in the lap. It emphasizes the posture's medicinal benefits and its role in awakening spiritual energy (Shakti) by uniting the body's internal breaths.</summary>

...and the <term>descending breath: apâna</term> in the navel, one attains the highest intelligence by awakening the <term>spiritual power: śakti</term> (the <term>coiled energy: kuṇḍalinî</term>) in this manner. 50.

*N.B.*—When the <term>descending breath: Apâna Vâyû</term> is drawn gently upward and after filling the lungs with air from outside, the <term>vital life-force: prâna</term> is forced downward gradually so as to join both of them at the navel; they both then enter the <term>coiled energy: Kundalinî</term> and, reaching the <term>Opening of Brahma: Brahma randhra</term> (the great aperture at the crown of the head), they make the mind calm. Then the mind can contemplate the nature of the <term>Self: âtman</term> and can enjoy the highest bliss.

The Yogî who, sitting with <term>Lotus Posture: Padmâsana</term>, can control breathing, there is no doubt, is free from bondage. 51.

### Now, the Lion Posture
<note>original: "atha siṃhāsanaṃ"</note>

->Place the heels below the scrotum on both sides of the seam of the<-
->perineum: the left heel on the right side and the right heel on the left. 52.<-
<note>original: "gulphau ca vṛṣaṇasyādhaḥ sīvanyāḥ pārśvayoḥ kṣipet | dakṣiṇe savyagulphaṃ tu dakṣagulphaṃ tu savyake || 52 ||"</note>

->*The Lion Posture.*<-

Press the heels on both sides of the seam of the <term>perineum: Perineum</term>, in such a way that the left heel touches the right side and the right heel touches the left side of it. 52.

->Place the hands upon the knees, spreading the fingers wide;<-
->with an open mouth and a collected mind, gaze at the tip of the nose. 53.<-
<note>original: "hastau tu jānvoḥ saṃsthāpya svāṅgulīḥ saṃprasārya ca | vyāttavaktro nirīkṣeta nāsāgraṃ susamāhitaḥ || 53 ||"</note>

Place the hands on the thighs, with stretched fingers, and keeping the mouth open and the mind collected, gaze on the tip of the nose. 53.

->This is the Lion Posture, held sacred by the greatest of yogis.<-
->This excellent posture facilitates the completion of the three locks. 54.<-
<note>original: "siṃhāsanaṃ bhavedetatpūjitaṃ yogipuṅgavaiḥ | bandhatritayasandhānaṃ kurute cāsanottamam || 54 ||"</note>
<note>The "three locks" refer to Mûlabandha (root lock), Jâlandhara Bandha (throat lock), and Uddîyâna Bandha (abdominal lock), which are used to seal energy within the body.</note>

This is <term>Lion Posture: Simhâsana</term>, held sacred by the best of Yogis. This excellent Posture effects the completion of the three <term>locks: Bandhas</term> (The <term>root lock: Mûlabandha</term>, <term>throat lock: Kantha or Jâlandhar Bandha</term> and <term>abdominal lock: Uddîyâna Bandha</term>). 54.

### Now, the Auspicious Posture
<note>original: "atha bhadrāsanaṃ"</note>

->Place the heels below the scrotum on both sides of the seam of the<-
->perineum: the left heel on the left side and the right heel on the right side. 55.<-
->Firmly grasping the sides of the feet with the hands, remain steady.<-
->This is the Auspicious Posture, the destroyer of all diseases. 56.<-
<note>original: "gulphau ca vṛṣaṇasyādhaḥ sīvanyāḥ pārśvayoḥ kṣipet | savyagulphaṃ tathā savye dakṣagulphaṃ tu dakṣiṇe || 55 || pārśvapādau ca pāṇibhyāṃ dṛḍhaṃ badhvā suniścitam | bhadrāsanaṃ bhavedetatsarvavyādhivināśanam || 56 ||"</note>

->*The Auspicious Posture.*<-

Place the heels on either side of the seam of the <term>perineum: Perineum</term>, keeping the left heel on the left side and the right one on the right side, hold the

...feet firmly joined to one another with both the hands. This <term>Auspicious Posture: Bhadrâsana</term> is the destroyer of all diseases. 55 and 56.

->The expert Yogîs call this the <term>Posture of Gorakṣa: Gorakṣa âsana</term>. By sitting in this posture, the master of Yoga gets rid of fatigue. 57.<-
<note>original: "gorakṣāsanamityāhuridaṃ vai siddhayoginaḥ || evamāsanabandheṣu yogīndro vigataśramaḥ || 57 ||"</note>

->The <term>energy channels: Nâdis</term> should be cleansed of their impurities by performing the <term>seals: mudrâs</term>, etc., (which are the practices relating to the movement of breath), <term>postures: Âsanas</term>, <term>breath retentions: Kumbhakas</term>, and various extraordinary <term>seals: mudrâs</term>. 58.<-

->By regular and close attention to the <term>inner sound: Nâda</term> (the <term>unstruck sound: anâhata nâda</term>) in Haṭha Yoga, a <term>celibate student: Brahmachari</term> who is sparing in diet, unattached to objects of enjoyment, and devoted to Yoga, gains success, without a doubt, within a year. 59.<-

-><term>Moderate diet: Mitâhâra</term> is that in which three-quarters of hunger is satisfied with food that is well-cooked with clarified butter (ghee) and sweets, and eaten as an offering to <term>the Lord of Yoga: Śiva</term>. 60.<-
<note>original: "susnigdhamadhurāhāraścaturthāṃśa vivarjitaḥ | bhujyate śivasaṃprītyai mitāhāraḥ sa ucyate || 60 ||"</note>

->कट्वम्ल तीक्ष्ण लवणोष्ण हरीत्शाक<-

->पिण्याक हिंगु लशुनाद्य मपथ्य माहुः ॥ ६१ ॥<-
<note>original Sanskrit listing prohibited items for the student of Yoga</note>

->*Foods injurious to a Yogî.*<-

->Bitter, sour, saltish, hot, green vegetables, fermented liquids, oily substances, foods mixed with sesame seed or mustard seed, intoxicating liquors, fish, meat, curds, <term>buttermilk: chhaasa</term>, certain pulses, plums, oil-cake, <term>asafœtida: hinga</term>, garlic, onion, etc., should not be eaten. 61.<-

->One should know that food which is reheated, dry, excessively salty, or sour, as well as poor-quality grains and vegetables that cause a burning sensation, must be avoided.<-

# THE LIGHT ON HATHA YOGA
<note>original title: "The Hatha Yoga Pradipika"</note>

...Lord Shiva. 62.
<note>Continuing from the previous page: "eaten as an offering to the Lord Shiva."</note>

->As stated by the master <term>Gorakṣa: a legendary founding sage of Hatha Yoga</term>:<-
<note>original: "tathāhi gorakṣavacanaṃ"</note>

One should avoid the company of wicked people, proximity to fire, the company of women, long journeys, early morning baths, fasting, and all activities that cause physical exhaustion. 63.
<note>original: "varjayeddurjanaprāntavahni strī pathisevanam || prātaḥsnānopavāsādi kāyakleśavidhiṃ tathā || 63 ||"</note>

Wheat, rice, barley, and <term>ṣāṣṭika: a nutritious rice that ripens in sixty days</term> are considered wholesome grains. Milk, <term>ghee: clarified butter</term>, brown sugar, butter, sugar-candy, honey, dried ginger, the <term>patola: a pointed gourd vegetable</term>, the five leafy vegetables, <term>mung beans: small green legumes</term>, and pure water are highly beneficial for the masters of <term>self-restraint: Yama</term> who practice Yoga. 64.
<note>original: "godhūma śāli yava ṣāṣṭikaśobhanānnaṃ | kṣīrājyakhaṇḍa navanīta sitāmadhūni || śuṃṭhīpaṭolakaphalādika pañca śākaṃ || mudgādidivyāmudakaṃ ca yamīndrapathyam || 64 ||"</note>

A Yogi should consume food that is nourishing, sweet, lubricated with fats or <term>ghee: clarified butter</term>, derived from cow’s milk, and capable of nourishing the <term>tissues: dhātus</term> of the body. One should eat what is pleasing to the mind and suitable for the practice. 65.
<note>original: "puṣṭaṃ sumadhuraṃ snigdhaṃ gavyaṃ dhātuprapoṣaṇam || manobhilaṣitaṃ yogyaṃ yogī bhojanamācaret || 65 ||"</note>

->Whether one is young, old, extremely aged, sick, or even weak,<-

->Success belongs to the one who is engaged in practice. How could it belong to the inactive?<-
->The <term>perfection of Yoga: Yoga-siddhi</term> is not achieved by merely reading the scriptures. 67.<-
<note>original: "kriyāyuktasya siddhiḥ syādakriyasya kathaṃ bhavet || na śāstrapāṭhamātreṇa yogasiddhiḥ prajāyate || 67 ||"</note>

->Wearing a specific costume is not the cause of success, nor is talking about it.<-
->Practice alone is the cause of success; this is the truth, without a doubt. 68.<-
<note>original: "na veṣadhāraṇaṃ siddheḥ kāraṇaṃ na ca tatkathā || kriyāiva kāraṇaṃ siddheḥ satyametanna saṃśayaḥ || 68 ||"</note>

->Postures, various <term>breath retentions: kumbhakas</term>, and extraordinary <term>physical seals: mudrâs</term><-
->are all practiced in the system of Haṭha until the fruit of <term>Royal Yoga: Râja Yoga, the ultimate state of meditative union</term> is achieved. 69.<-

# CHAPTER II

->*On Breath Control*<-
<note>original: "Prâṇâyâma"</note>

# SECOND INSTRUCTION
<note>original: "dvitiyopadeśaḥ"</note>

<note>Institutional stamps are visible at the top left and right corners of the original manuscript page.</note>

->Once the posture is firmly established, the <term>Yogi: a practitioner of yoga</term>, who has mastered his senses and consumes wholesome, moderate food, should practice <term>Pranayama: the regulation of breath</term> according to the method taught by his <term>Guru: spiritual teacher</term>. 1.<-

->When the breath moves, the mind also moves; when the breath is still, the mind becomes still. The practitioner attains a state of steadiness; therefore, one should restrain the breath. 2.<-
<note>The text highlights the ancient Indian psychological insight that respiratory patterns and mental states are mirrors of one another.</note>

->As long as the vital air remains in the body, it is called "life." Death is the departure of that air. Therefore, one should restrain the breath. 3.<-

->When the <term>Nadis: the subtle energy channels of the body</term> are choked with impurities, the breath cannot pass into the <term>Sushumna: the central channel of the subtle body</term>. How then can the state of <term>Unmani Avastha: a high state of meditative absorption beyond the mind</term> be reached? How can success be achieved? 4.<-

->Only when the entire network of channels, which was filled with impurities, is purified, does the practitioner become capable of controlling the <term>Prana: the vital life force</term>. 5.<-

->Therefore, breath control should be performed daily with a <term>Sattvic: pure and balanced</term> intellect, so that the impurities within the central channel are cleansed. 6.<-
<note>A "Sattvic" mind is one free from "Rajas" (agitation/activity) and "Tamas" (lethargy/sloth).</note>

<summary>This chapter introduces Pranayama, explaining that the mind's stability depends entirely on the stability of the breath. It posits that the body's energy channels must be purified before a practitioner can successfully direct their life force into the central spiritual channel.</summary>

<keywords>Breath Control, Pranayama, Nadis, Sushumna, Vital Force, Mental Steadiness, Purification, Sattvic Intellect</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the "Second Instruction" on Pranayama (breath control) from the previous page. It moves from the theory of purification to the specific practical steps of alternate nostril breathing, detailing the technique, the frequency of practice, and the physical signs of progress.</meta>

# THE HATHA YOGA PRADIPIKA

<meta>Institutional stamps are present at the top left and right corners of the original page.</meta>

> <note>original: "baddhapadmāsano yogī prāṇaṃ candreṇa pūrayet | dhārayitvā yathāśakti bhūyaḥ sūryeṇa recayet || 7 || prāṇaṃ sūryeṇa cākṛṣyapūrayedudaraṃ śanaiḥ | vidhivatkumbhakaṃ kṛtvā punascandreṇa recayet || 8 ||"</note>
> The Yogi, sitting in the <term>Padmasana: the lotus posture</term>, should draw in the <term>Prana: the vital life force</term> through the <term>Chandra: the "moon" or left nostril</term>. Having held it according to his capacity, he should then exhale through the <term>Surya: the "sun" or right nostril</term>. Then, drawing in the breath through the right nostril slowly, he should fill the abdomen; and after performing <term>Kumbhaka: breath retention</term> according to the rules, he should exhale again through the left nostril. 7 and 8.

->*Method of performing <term>Pranayama: breath regulation</term>.*<-

Sitting in the <term>Padmasana</term> posture, the practitioner should fill the lungs with air through the left nostril (while closing the right one); and, keeping it held according to one's ability, it should be expelled slowly through the right nostril. Then, drawing in the air through the right nostril slowly, the belly should be filled, and after performing breath retention as before, it should be expelled slowly through the left nostril. 7 and 8.

> <note>original: "yena tyajettena pītvā dhārayedatirodhataḥ | recayecca tato'nyena śanaireva na vegataḥ || 9 ||"</note>
> Inhaling through the same nostril that was used for exhalation, and having restrained the breath to the limit of one's endurance, one should exhale through the other nostril slowly and not with force. 9.

Inhaling thus through the nostril through which the air was just expelled, and having restrained it there as long as possible, it should be exhaled through the opposite nostril, slowly and not forcibly. 9.

> <note>original: "prāṇaṃ cedidayā pibenniyamitaṃ bhūyo'nyayā recayet pītvā piṅgalayā samīraṇamatho baddhvātyajedvāmayā | sūryācandramasoranena vidhinābhyāsaṃ sadā tanvatāṃ śuddhā nāḍigaṇā bhavanti yamināṃ māsatrayādūrdhvataḥ || 10 ||"</note>
> If the air is inhaled through the <term>Ida: the left energy channel</term>, it should be expelled through the other. After inhaling through the <term>Pingala: the right energy channel</term> and holding the breath, it should be released through the left. By constantly practicing this method of alternate breathing through the "sun" and "moon" channels, the entire network of <term>Nadis: subtle energy channels</term> of the <term>Yamis: self-controlled practitioners</term> becomes purified after three months of practice. 10.

If the air be inhaled through the left nostril, it should be expelled again through the other, and filling it through the right nostril, confining it there, it should be expelled through the left nostril. By practicing in this way, through the right and the left nostrils alternately, the whole collection of the energy channels of the practitioners becomes clean—that is, free from impurities—after three months and beyond. 10.

> <note>original: "prātarmadhyandine sāyamardharātre ca kumbhakān | śanairaśītiparyantaṃ caturvāraṃ samabhyaset || 11 ||"</note>
> One should practice <term>Kumbhaka</term> (breath retention) four times a day: in the morning, at midday, in the evening, and at midnight, gradually increasing until the number of retentions reaches eighty per session. 11.

Breath retentions should be performed gradually four times during the day and night (morning, noon, evening, and midnight), until the number of retentions for a single session is 80, totaling 320 for the entire day and night. 11.

> <note>original: "kanīyasi bhavetsveda kampo bhavati madhyame | uttame sthānamāpnoti tato vāyuṃ nibandhayet || 12 ||"</note>
> In the lowest stage of practice, there is perspiration; in the middle stage, there is quivering of the body; and in the highest stage, one reaches a state of stillness. Therefore, one should control the breath. 12.

In the beginning stage of practice, there is perspiration; in the middle stage, there is a quivering or trembling; and in the final or third stage, one obtains steadiness or levitation; and then the breath should be made steady and motionless. 12.

<note>The stages described in verse 12 refer to the physical markers of "purification" and the rising of internal energy (Prana). Perspiration is seen as the clearing of physical impurities, while quivering indicates the activation of the nervous system.</note>

<summary>This page outlines the specific technique of Nadi Shodhana (alternate nostril breathing), prescribing a rigorous schedule of 320 retentions daily to purify the body's energy channels over three months.</summary>
<keywords>Pranayama, Nadi Shodhana, Padmasana, Kumbhaka, Ida, Pingala, Purification, Yoga stages</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the "Second Instruction" on Pranayama (breath control). It shifts from the technical mechanics of alternate nostril breathing to the physical care of the body, dietary guidelines, and the vital necessity of a gradual, disciplined approach to avoid physical harm.</meta>

# THE HATHA YOGA PRADIPIKA

<meta>Institutional stamps are visible at the top left and right corners of the original page.</meta>

-><note>original: "jalena śramajātena gātramardanamācaret || dṛḍhatā laghutā caiva tena gātrasya jāyate || 13 ||"</note><-
->One should massage the body with the perspiration arising from the exertion of practice.<-
->By this, the body attains firmness and lightness. 13.<-

The perspiration exuding from the exertion of practice should be rubbed into the body (and not wiped away), as by doing so the body becomes strong. 13.

-><note>original: "abhyāsakāle prathame śastaṃ kṣīrājyabhojanam || tato'bhyāse dṛḍhībhūte na tādṛṅ niyamagrahaḥ || 14 ||"</note><-
->During the initial stage of practice, a diet of milk and <term>ghee: clarified butter often used in sacred rituals and health</term> is recommended.<-
->Once the practice is firmly established, such strict rules need not be followed. 14.<-

During the first stage of practice, food consisting of milk and ghee is wholesome. When the practice becomes established, no such restriction is necessary. 14.

-><note>original: "yathā siṃho gajo vyāghro bhavedvaśyaḥ śanaiḥ śanaiḥ || tathaiva sevito vāyuranyathā hanti sādhakam || 15 ||"</note><-
->Just as lions, elephants, and tigers are brought under control very gradually,<-
->so too should the breath be controlled; otherwise, it destroys the practitioner. 15.<-

Just as lions, elephants and tigers are controlled by and by, so the breath is controlled by slow degrees, otherwise (that is, by being hasty or using too much force) it kills the practitioner himself. 15.

-><note>original: "prāṇāyāmādiyuktena sarvarogakṣayo bhavet || ayuktābhyāsayogena sarvarogasamudbhavaḥ || 16 ||"</note><-
->Through the proper practice of <term>Pranayama: breath regulation</term> and other techniques, all diseases are eradicated.<-
->Through improper practice, all diseases are generated. 16.<-

When <term>Pranayama</term>, etc., are performed properly, they eradicate all diseases; but an improper practice generates diseases. 16.

-><note>original: "hikkā śvāsaśca kāsaśca śiraḥkarṇākṣivedanāḥ || bhavanti vividhā rogāḥ pavanasya prakopataḥ || 17 ||"</note><-
->Hiccups, asthma, coughs, and pains in the head, ears, and eyes;<-
->various diseases such as these arise from the provocation of the breath. 17.<-

Hiccough, asthma, cough, pain in the head, the ears, and the eyes; these and other various kinds of diseases are generated by the disturbance of the breath. 17.

-><note>original: "yuktaṃ yuktaṃ tyajedvāyuṃ yuktaṃ yuktaṃ ca pūrayet || yuktaṃ yuktaṃ ca badhnīyādevaṃ siddhimavāpnuyāt || 18 ||"</note><-
->One should exhale the breath with skill, inhale it with skill,<-
->and restrain it with skill; thus one attains success. 18.<-

The air should be expelled with proper tact and should be filled in skillfully; and when it has been kept confined properly it brings success. 18.

Note—<note>original: "N.B."</note> The above caution is necessary to warn the aspirants against omitting any instruction; and, in their zeal to gain success or <term>siddhis: spiritual or supernatural accomplishments</term> early, to begin the practice either by using too much force in filling in, confining, and expelling the air, or by omitting any instructions, which may cause unnecessary pressure on their ears, eyes, etc., and cause pain. Every word in the instructions is full of meaning and is necessarily used in the <term>slokas: poetic verses or couplets</term>, and should be followed very carefully and with due attention. Thus there will be nothing to fear whatsoever. We are inhaling and exhaling the air throughout our lives without any sort of danger, and <term>Pranayama</term> being only a regular form of it, there should be no cause to fear.

<summary>This page details the physical requirements for Yoga practice, including using perspiration to strengthen the body and consuming a diet of milk and ghee. It strongly emphasizes a gradual, skillful approach to breath control, warning that improper technique can cause serious respiratory and sensory ailments.</summary>
<keywords>Hatha Yoga Pradipika, Pranayama, breath control, yoga diet, ghee, physical health, yoga safety, siddhis, slokas</keywords>
