# THE HATHA YOGA PRADIPIKA.

<meta>Institutional stamps are visible at the top left and right corners of the original page.</meta>

-><note>original: "yadā tu nāḍīśuddhiḥ syāttathā cihnāni bāhyataḥ || kāyasya kṛśatā kāntistadā jāyeta niścitam || 19 ||"</note><-
->When the <term>nāḍīs: subtle energy channels through which life force flows</term> become free from impurities, and the outward signs of success appear—such as a lean body and a glowing complexion—then one should feel certain of success. 19.<-

-><note>original: "yatheṣṭadhāraṇaṃ vāyoranalasya pradīpanam || nādābhivyakti rārogyaṃ jāyate nāḍīśodhanāt || 20 ||"</note><-
->By removing the impurities, the breath can be restrained according to one's wish, the appetite <note>literally "the inner fire"</note> is increased, the divine sound <note>the 'nada' or internal vibration heard in deep meditation</note> is awakened, and the body becomes healthy. 20.<-

-><note>original: "medaśleṣmādhikaḥ pūrvaṃ ṣaṭ karmāṇi samācaret || anyastu nācarettāni doṣāṇāṃ samabhāvataḥ || 21 ||"</note><-
->If there be an excess of fat or phlegm in the body, the six kinds of <term>kriyās: cleansing duties or actions</term> should be performed first. But others, not suffering from an excess of these <term>doshas: bodily humors</term>, should not perform them. 21.<-

-><note>original: "dhautirbastistathā netistrāṭakaṃ naulikaṃ tathā || kapālabhātiścaitāni ṣaṭ karmāṇi pracakṣate || 22 ||"</note><-
->The six kinds of duties are: <gloss>stomach cleansing</gloss> Dhauti, <gloss>yogic enema</gloss> Basti, <gloss>nasal cleansing</gloss> Neti, <gloss>steady gazing</gloss> Trāṭaka, <gloss>abdominal churning</gloss> Nauli, and <gloss>skull-shining breath</gloss> Kapālabhāti. These are called the <term>Shatkarma: the six actions</term>. 22.<-

-><note>original: "karmaṣaṭkamidaṃ gopyaṃ ghaṭaśodhanakārakam || vicitraguṇasandhāyi pūjyate yogipuṅgavaiḥ || 23 ||"</note><-
->These six kinds of actions which cleanse the body <note>literally "purify the vessel"</note> should be kept secret. They produce extraordinary attributes and are performed with earnestness by the best of Yogis. 23.<-

-><note>original: "tatra dhautiḥ ||"</note><-
->**The Dhauti (Stomach Cleansing)**<-

-><note>original: "caturaṅgulavistāraṃ hastapañcadaśāyatam || gurūpadiṣṭamārgeṇa siktaṃ vastraṃ śanairgraset || punaḥ pratyāhareccaitaduditaṃ dhautikarma tat || 24 ||"</note><-
->A strip of cloth, about four fingers wide and fifteen cubits <note>A cubit is approximately 18 inches, the length of a forearm; this refers to a cloth roughly 22 feet long.</note> long, is swallowed slowly while moist with warm water, following the method shown by the <term>guru: teacher</term>, and is then taken out again. This is called the Dhauti action. 24.<-

*N.B.*—The strip should be moistened with a little warm water, and the end should be held with the teeth. It is swallowed slowly, little by little: for example, the first day 1 cubit, the 2nd day 2 cubits, the 3rd day 3 cubits, and so on. After swallowing it, the stomach should be given a vigorous circular motion from left to right, and then the cloth should be withdrawn slowly and gently.

<gloss>nāḍīs: energy channels</gloss>, <gloss>kriyās: cleansing practices</gloss>, <gloss>Shatkarma: the six-fold purification</gloss>, <gloss>Dhauti: internal washing</gloss>, <gloss>Basti: colon cleansing</gloss>, <gloss>Neti: nasal irrigation</gloss>, <gloss>Trāṭaka: concentrated staring</gloss>, <gloss>Nauli: abdominal isolation</gloss>, <gloss>Kapāla Bhāti: respiratory purification</gloss>, <gloss>Yogis: practitioners of yoga</gloss>, <gloss>guru: spiritual master</gloss>

<summary>This page describes the physical signs of success in yoga, such as a radiant complexion and lean body, and introduces the 'Shatkarma' or six cleansing techniques. It specifically details the practice of 'Dhauti,' an internal cleansing of the stomach using a long strip of cloth.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the Second Instruction of the Hatha Yoga Pradipika, focusing on the Shatkarmas (six purification actions). It concludes the section on Dhauti (stomach cleansing) and introduces Basti (yogic enema) and Neti (nasal cleansing).</meta>

# THE HATHA YOGA PRADIPIKA.

<sig>D</sig>

<meta>Institutional stamps are visible at the top left and right corners. The paper shows age-related discoloration and fold lines.</meta>

-><note>original: "kāsa-śvāsa-plīha-kuṣṭhaṃ kapha-rogaś-ca viṃśatiḥ || dhauti-karma-prabhāvena prayānty-eva na saṃśayaḥ || 25 ||"</note><-
->There is no doubt that cough, asthma, enlargement of the spleen, leprosy, and twenty varieties of diseases born of <term>kapha: the phlegm or mucus humor</term> disappear through the power of <term>Dhauti: the practice of internal cleansing</term>. 25.<-

-><note>original: "nābhidaghna-jale pāyau nyasta-nālotkaṭāsanaḥ || ādhārākuñcanaṃ kuryāt-kṣālanaṃ basti-karma tat || 26 ||"</note><-

->**The Basti** <gloss>Yogic Enema</gloss>.<-

->Squatting in navel-deep water in <term>utkatasana: a chair-like squatting pose</term>, one should insert a smooth tube <note>traditionally a hollow bamboo or reed about six inches long</note> into the anus. By contracting the anal sphincter, the water is drawn up and then expelled. This washing is called <term>Basti Karma</term>. 26.<-

-><note>original: "gulma-plīhodaraṃ cāpi vāta-pitta-kapho-dbhavāḥ || basti-karma-prabhāvena kṣīyante sakalā-mayāḥ || 27 ||"</note><-
->By practicing this Basti Karma, abdominal tumors, an enlarged spleen, and dropsy—as well as all disorders arising from the imbalances of <term>Vata, Pitta, and Kapha: the three bodily humors of air, bile, and phlegm</term>—are cured. 27.<-

-><note>original: "dhātv-indriyāntaḥ-karaṇa-prasādaṃ dadyāc-ca kānti-dahana-pradīptim || aśeṣa-doṣopacayaṃ nihanyād-abhyasyamānaṃ jala-basti-karma || 28 ||"</note><-
->By practicing Basti with water, the <term>dhatus: the seven fundamental tissues of the body</term>, the <term>indriyas: the sensory organs</term>, and the mind become clear and calm. It grants a healthy glow to the body, kindles the digestive fire <note>appetite and metabolic heat</note>, and destroys the accumulation of all bodily disorders. 28.<-

->**Now, Neti** <gloss>Nasal Cleansing</gloss>.<-

-><note>original: "sūtraṃ vitasti-susnigdhaṃ nāsā-nāle praveśayet || mukhād-virgamaye-ccaiṣā netiḥ siddhair-nigadyate || 29 ||"</note><-
->One should insert a smooth cord, about <gloss>a span or nine inches</gloss> long, through the nasal passage and pull the end out through the mouth. This is called <term>Neti</term> by the <term>Siddhas: perfected masters of yoga</term>. 29.<-

-><note>original: "kapāla-śodhinī caiva divya-dṛṣṭi-pradāyinī || jatrūrdhva-jāta-rogaughaṃ netir-āśu nihanti ca || 30 ||"</note><-
->Neti cleanses the skull and bestows <gloss>divine or clairvoyant</gloss> sight. It rapidly destroys all diseases that occur in the regions above the collarbone <note>the neck, throat, and head</note>. 30.

<summary>This page details the physical benefits and techniques of Dhauti, Basti, and Neti, three of the six yogic purification methods used to balance the body's humors and prepare for advanced breath control.</summary>
<keywords>Shatkarma, Dhauti, Basti, Neti, Doshas, Kapha, Vata, Pitta, purification, yogic hygiene</keywords>

# THE HATHA YOGA PRADIPIKA.

<meta>Institutional stamps are visible at the top left and right corners.</meta>

->**Now, Trataka.**<-

-><note>original: "nirīkṣen-niścala-dṛśā sūkṣma-lakṣyaṃ samāhitaḥ | aśru-sampāta-paryantam ācāryais-trāṭakaṃ smṛtam | 31 |"</note><-
->Being calm and concentrated, one should gaze steadily with an unblinking eye at a small mark until tears begin to flow. This is known as <term>Trataka: the practice of steady gazing</term> by the <term>acharyas: learned teachers or masters</term>. 31.<-

-><note>original: "mocanaṃ netra-rogāṇāṃ tandrā-dīnāṃ kapāṭakam | yatnatas-trāṭakaṃ gopyaṃ yathā hāṭaka-peṭakam | 32 |"</note><-
->Trataka cures eye diseases and acts as a "door-bolt" to shut out sloth and drowsiness. It should be kept secret with great effort, like a box of gold jewelry. 32.<-

->**Now, Nauli.**<-

-><note>original: "amanda-āvarta-vegena tundaṃ savyāpasavyataḥ | natāṃso bhrāmayed-eṣā nauliḥ siddhaiḥ pracakṣyate | 33 |"</note><-

->***The Nauli (Abdominal Churning).***<-

->With the shoulders bent forward, one should rotate the abdomen from left to right with the speed of a rapid whirlpool. This is called <term>Nauli: a core-strengthening and cleansing movement</term> by the <term>siddhas: perfected masters</term>. <note>The source text notes a specific posture: sitting on the toes with heels raised and palms on the ground, though the verse focuses on the "whirlpool" motion of the belly.</note> 33.<-

-><note>original: "mandāgni-sandīpana-pācanādi sandhāpikā nanda-karī sadaiva | aśeṣa-doṣāmaya-śoṣaṇī ca haṭha-kriyā-maulir-iyaṃ ca nauliḥ | 34 |"</note><-
->It stimulates a weak digestive fire, aids digestion, and always brings a sense of happiness. It dries up all humors and diseases. This Nauli is the crown of all <term>Hatha Yoga</term> practices. 34.<-

->**Now, Kapala Bhati.**<-

-><note>original: "bhastravallohakārasya recapūrau sasaṃbhramau | kapālabhātir-vikhyātā kaphadoṣaviśoṣaṇī | 35 |"</note><-

->***The Kapala Bhati (Skull-Shining Breath).***<-

->When exhalation and inhalation are performed rapidly like the bellows of a blacksmith, it is known as <term>Kapala Bhati: "the shining skull" cleansing breath</term>. It dries up all disorders caused by an excess of <term>kapha: the phlegm or mucus humor</term>. 35.<-

-><note>original: "ṣaṭ-karma-nirgata-sthaulya-kapha-doṣa-malādikaḥ | prāṇāyāmaṃ tataḥ kuryād-anāyāsena siddhyati | 36 |"</note><-
->Once obesity, phlegm, and other impurities have been removed by these <term>Shatkarmas: the six purification duties</term>, one should then perform <term>Pranayama: breath control</term>. Success will then be achieved without difficulty. 36.

<summary>This page describes the final three purification techniques of Hatha Yoga: Trataka (gazing), Nauli (abdominal churning), and Kapala Bhati (rapid breathing). These "Shatkarmas" prepare the body for successful breath control by removing physical impurities and balancing the humors.</summary>
<keywords>Trataka, Nauli, Kapala Bhati, Shatkarma, Hatha Yoga, purification, digestive fire, phlegm, Pranayama</keywords>

-><note>original: "prāṇāyāmaireva sarve praśuṣyanti malā iti | ācāryāṇāṃ tu keṣāñcidanyatkarma na sammatam | 37 |"</note><-
->Some <term>acharyas: learned teachers or masters</term> do not advocate for any other practice, being of the opinion that all impurities are dried up by the practice of <term>Pranayama: the formal regulation of breath</term> alone. 37.<-

### Now, Gaja Karani (The Elephant Technique)

-><note>original: "udara gatapadārthamudvamanti pavanamapānamudīrya kaṇṭhanāle | kramaparicaya vaśyanāḍicakrā gajakaraṇīti nigadyate haṭhajñaiḥ | 38 |"</note><-
->***The Gaja Karani (Elephant Gesture).***<-

->By drawing the <term>Apana Vayu: the downward-flowing vital energy</term> up to the throat, the contents of the stomach are vomited. Through gradual practice, the system of <term>nadis: energy channels, specifically the Shankhini</term> comes under control. This is called <term>Gaja Karani</term> by those who know the secrets of <term>Hatha: the yoga of physical discipline</term>. 38.<-

-><note>original: "brahmādayo'pi tridaśāḥ pavanābhyāsa tatparāḥ | abhūvannantakabhayāttasmātpavana mabhyaset | 39 |"</note><-
->Even <term>Brahma: the creator god</term> and other <term>Devas: celestial beings</term> were always devoted to the practice of breath control because they feared death. Therefore, one should practice the regulation of breath regularly. 39.<-

-><note>original: "yāvadbaddho maruddehe yāvaccittaṃ nirākulam | yāvaddṛṣṭirbhruvormadhye tāvatkālabhayaṃ kutaḥ | 40 |"</note><-
->So long as the breath is held within the body, so long as the mind is undisturbed, and so long as the gaze is fixed between the eyebrows, how can there be any fear of Death? 40.<-

-><note>original: "vidhivatprāṇasaṃyāmairnāḍīcakre viśodhite | suṣumnāvadanaṃ bhittvā sukhādviśati mārutaḥ | 41 |"</note><-
->When the system of energy channels is cleared of impurities by properly controlling the breath, then the air, piercing the entrance of the <term>Sushumna: the central energy channel of the spine</term>, enters it with ease. 41.<-

### Now, Manonmani (The State of Mind-Beyond-Mind)

-><note>original: "mārute madhyasañcāre manaḥsthairyaṃ prajāyate | yo manaḥsusthirībhāvaḥ saivāvasthā manonmanī | 42 |"</note><-
->***Manonmani.***<-

->Steadiness of mind arises when the breath moves freely through the middle channel. That state of perfect mental stability is known as <term>Manonmani: the condition where the mind transcends its ordinary functions</term>. 42.<-

<summary>This page argues that breath control alone can purify the body and describes Gaja Karani, a technique for cleansing the stomach, while explaining how mastering the breath leads to the spiritual state of Manonmani.</summary>
<keywords>Pranayama, Gaja Karani, Apana Vayu, Nadis, Sushumna, Manonmani, liberation from death, purification, Hatha Yoga</keywords>

# THE HATHA YOGA PRADIPIKA.

<meta>Institutional stamps are visible at the top left and right corners of the original page.</meta>

-><note>original: "tat-siddhaye vidhānajñāś-citrān kurvanti kumbhakān || vicitra-kumbhakābhyāsād-vicitrāṃ siddhim āpnuyāt || 43 ||"</note><-
->To accomplish it, various <term>Kumbhakas: techniques of breath retention</term> are performed by those who are expert in the methods; for, by the practice of different Kumbhakas, wonderful success is attained. 43.<-

### Now, the Varieties of Breath Retention

-><note>original: "sūryabhedanamujjāyī sītkārī śītalī tathā || bhastrikā bhrāmarī mūrchā plāvanītyaṣṭa kumbhakāḥ || 44 ||"</note><-

->***Different kinds of breath retentions.***<-

-><term>Kumbhakas</term> are of eight kinds, namely: <term>Surya Bhedan: sun-piercing breath</term>, <term>Ujjayi: victorious breath</term>, <term>Sitkari: hissing breath</term>, <term>Sitali: cooling breath</term>, <term>Bhastrika: bellows breath</term>, <term>Bhramari: humming bee breath</term>, <term>Murchha: swooning breath</term>, and <term>Plavini: floating breath</term>. 44.<-

-><note>original: "pūrakānte tu kartavyo bandho jālandharābhidhaḥ || kumbhakānte recakādau kartavyastūḍḍiyānakaḥ || 45 ||"</note><-

->At the end of <term>Puraka: inhalation</term>, the <term>Jalandhara Bandha: the throat lock</term> should be performed; and at the end of <term>Kumbhaka: retention</term>, and at the beginning of <term>Rechaka: exhalation</term>, the <term>Uddiyana Bandha: the abdominal contraction</term> should be performed. 45.<-

<note>Translator's Note: Puraka is the process of filling in the air from outside. Kumbhaka is the act of keeping the air confined inside. Rechaka is the act of expelling the confined air. Detailed instructions for these phases of breathing will be found later in the text and should be followed with great care.</note>

-><note>original: "adhastātkuñcanenāśu kaṇṭhasaṅkocane kṛte || madhye paścimatānena syātprāṇo brahmanāḍigaḥ || 46 ||"</note><-

->By drawing up from below through <term>Mula Bandha: the root lock</term>, contracting the throat with <term>Jalandhara Bandha</term>, and pulling back the middle of the front portion of the body—the belly—the <term>Prana: vital life force</term> enters the <term>Brahma Nadi: the divine channel, also known as the Sushumna</term>. 46.<-

<note>The "middle hole" or central channel through the vertebral column, through which the spinal energy passes, is called the Sushumna Nadi by the Yogis. The two other vital channels, located on either side of the spinal cord, are known as the Ida and the Pingala Nadis. These energy pathways will be described in more detail in later sections.</note>

-><note>original: "apānamūrdhvamutthāpya prāṇaṃ kaṇṭhādadho nayet || yogī jarāviyuktaḥ san ṣoḍaśābdavayo bhavet || 47 ||"</note><-

->By pulling up the <term>Apana Vayu: the downward-moving energy</term> and by forcing the <term>Prana Vayu: the upward-moving energy</term> down from the throat, the Yogi, liberated from the effects of old age, becomes youthful, appearing as if they were only sixteen years old. 47.<-

Note on the Five Vital Airs: <note>original: "hṛdi prāṇo gude'pānaḥ samāno nābhimaṇḍale | udānaḥ kaṇṭhadeśastho vyānaḥ sarvaśarīragaḥ | śivasaṃhitāyām a. 3 ślo. 7 |"</note>

The seat of the **Prana** is the heart; of the **Apana**, the anus; of the **Samana**, the region around the navel; of the **Udana**, the throat; while the **Vyana** moves throughout the entire body. —*From the Shiva Samhita, Chapter 3, Verse 7.*

<summary>This page introduces the eight specific types of Kumbhaka (breath retention) and the application of Bandhas (physical locks) to direct vital energy into the central Sushumna channel for health and spiritual success. It also defines the locations of the five primary vital airs (Vayus) within the human body according to the Shiva Samhita.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the "Second Instruction" of the Hatha Yoga Pradipika, detailing the first of the eight types of Kumbhaka (breath retention), Surya Bhedana, and providing a significant supplementary note on the daily routine and sequence of practice for a yogi.</meta>

# THE HATHA YOGA PRADIPIKA.

<meta>Institutional stamps are visible at the top left and right corners of the original page.</meta>

### Now, Surya Bhedana (The Sun-Piercing Breath)

-><note>original: "āsane sukhade yogī baddhvā caivāsanaṃ tataḥ | dakṣanāḍyā samākṛṣya bahiḥsthaṃ pavanaṃ śanaiḥ || 48 ||"</note><-
->Taking any comfortable posture and performing the <term>Asana: physical posture</term>, the <term>Yogi: practitioner</term> should draw in the air slowly through the right nostril. 48.<-

->***Surya Bhedana* (The Sun-Piercing Breath).**<-

Taking any comfortable posture and performing the Asana, the Yogi should draw in the air slowly, through the right nostril. 48.

-><note>original: "ākeśādānakhāgrācca nirodhāvadhi kumbhayet | tataḥ śanaiḥ savyanāḍyā recayetpavanaṃ śanaiḥ || 49 ||"</note><-
->Then it should be confined within, so that it fills from the nails to the tips of the hair, and then let out through the left nostril slowly. 49.<-

Then it should be confined within, so that it fills from the nails to the tips of the hair, and then let out through the left nostril slowly. 49.

<note>Note.—This is to be done alternately with both the nostrils, drawing in through the one, expelling through the other, and vice versa.</note>

-><note>original: "kapālaśodhanaṃ vātadoṣaghnaṃ kṛmidoṣahṛt | punaḥ punaridaṃ kāryaṃ sūryabhedanamuttamam || 50 ||"</note><-
->This excellent Surya Bhedana cleanses the forehead (frontal sinuses), destroys the disorders of <term>Vata: the air or wind humor</term>, and removes worms, and, therefore, it should be performed again and again. 50.<-

This excellent Surya Bhedana cleanses the forehead (frontal sinuses), destroys the disorders of Vata, and removes the worms, and, therefore, it should be performed again and again. 50.

Note.—

<note>The following is a traditional sequence for the practice of Yoga:</note>

I shall state the sequence of Yoga practice for the success of Yogis. A wise man should rise at the time of dawn or in the early morning. 1.
Remembering the <term>Guru: teacher</term> at the crown of the head and his <term>Ishta-devata: chosen deity</term> in the heart, he should perform purification, clean his teeth, and apply sacred ashes. 2.
In a clean, pleasant <term>Matha: monastery or hermitage</term>, having established a soft seat, he should sit there and remember the Guru and God in his mind. 3.
Declaring the place and time, and making a formal <term>Sankalpa: ritual intention</term> according to the rules, he should say: "Today, beginning with this, for the sake of attaining the fruit of <term>Samadhi: meditative absorption</term> and the grace of the Supreme Lord, I shall perform <term>Pranayama: breath control</term> and other practices, preceded by Asanas." He should bow to the infinite God, the Lord of Serpents, to gain success in his seat. 4.
"Salutations to <term>Ananta: the infinite serpent</term>, the King of Nagas, whose thousand radiant hoods support the orb of the world." Then he should practice Asanas. If fatigue arises, he should perform <term>Shavasana: the corpse pose</term>. At the end of the practice, he should do so; however, if there is no fatigue, he need not do it. 5.
The practice called <term>Viparita Karani: inverted action</term> should be performed before <term>Kumbhaka: breath retention</term>. For the sake of the grace of <term>Jalandhara: the throat lock</term>, it should be done before the union of Kumbhaka. 6.
Having performed <term>Achamana: ritual sipping of water</term> as a limb of the rite of breath restraint, the Yogi should bow to the great lords of Yoga, following the words of Shiva. 7.
<margin>From the Kurma Purana.</margin>
Having bowed to the lords of Yoga, their disciples, <term>Vinayaka: Ganesha, the remover of obstacles</term>, and the Guru, the Yogi should then join himself in practice with a well-composed mind. 8.
When the practice of <term>Siddhasana: the accomplished pose</term> is mastered along with Kumbhaka and the <term>Bandhas: energy locks</term>, ten should be performed at first, increasing by five every day. 9.
Kumbhakas should be performed by the well-composed until they reach eighty. The great Yogi should first practice the breath of the "Moon and Sun" (alternate nostrils). 10.
The wise call this practice <term>Anuloma Viloma: alternate nostril breathing</term>. Having practiced Surya Bhedana with the locks and a one-pointed mind, 11.
He should then perform <term>Ujjayi: the victorious breath</term>, then <term>Sitkari: the hissing breath</term>, and then <term>Sitali: the cooling breath</term>. Having practiced <term>Bhastrika: the bellows breath</term>, he should perform other major <term>Mudras: ritual gestures or seals</term>. 12.
He should practice the Mudras in proper order as learned from the mouth of the Guru. Then, having bound himself in <term>Padmasana: the lotus pose</term>, he should perform the appropriate bodily movements. 13.
He should dedicate the entirety of his practice to God from the beginning. Rising from practice, he should bathe with warm water. 14.
Having bathed, the wise man should complete his daily duties briefly. He should practice again at midday, and after resting a little, take his meal. 15.

A yogi should always eat what is wholesome and never what is unwholesome. After a meal, one may chew <term>cardamom</term> or <term>cloves</term>. 16.

Some prefer camphor and fine <term>betel leaf</term>. For those practicing <term>breath control</term>, betel leaf without lime is recommended. 17.

After eating, one should contemplate the <term>scriptures of liberation</term>, listen to <term>ancient legends</term>, or chant the names of the Lord. 18.

Having performed the evening rites, practice yoga as before. When three <term>Ghatikas</term> <note>about 72 minutes</note> of daylight remain, begin the practice. 19.

The wise should always perform evening rites after practice. At midnight, the self-controlled yogi should practice Hatha as before. 20.

The <term>inverted practice</term> should be done at evening and midnight; it should not be practiced immediately after eating, as that is not advisable. 21.

->Translation: I am going to describe the procedure of the practice of Yoga, in order that Yogis may succeed. A wise man should leave his bed in the <term>Usha Kala: the time before dawn, roughly 4:00 AM</term> in the morning. 1.<-

Remembering his <term>guru: spiritual teacher</term> over his head, and his desired deity in his heart, after answering the calls of nature, and cleaning his mouth, he should apply <term>Bhasma: sacred ashes</term>. 2.

In a clean spot, clean room and charming ground, he should spread a soft <term>asana: a seat or mat</term>. Having seated on it and remembering in his mind his guru and his God. 3.

Having extolled the place and the time and taking up the vow thus: "Today by the grace of God, I will perform <term>Pranayama: breath control</term> with physical postures for gaining <term>samadhi: meditative absorption</term> and its fruits." He should salute the infinite <term>Deva: deity</term>, Lord of the <term>Nagas: semi-divine serpent beings</term>, to ensure success in the postures. 4.

Salutation to the Lord of the Nagas <note>likely referring to Ananta Shesha, the serpent who supports the world and is associated with Patanjali</note>, who is adorned with thousands of heads, set with brilliant jewels, and who has sustained the whole universe, nourishes it, and is infinite. After this he should begin his exercise of postures and when fatigued, he should practice the <term>Corpse Pose</term>. Should there be no fatigue, he should not practice it. 5.

Before <term>Kumbhaka: breath retention</term>, he should perform the <term>Viparita Karani: inverted action</term> gesture, in order that he may be able to perform the <term>Jalandhara Bandha: throat lock</term> comfortably. 6.

Sipping a little water, he should begin the exercise of breath control, after saluting the <term>Lords of the Yogis</term>, as described in the <term>Kurma Purana: an ancient sacred text</term>, in the words of the god Shiva. 7.

Such as "Saluting the Lords of the Yogis and their disciples and the teacher <term>Vinayaka: the god Ganesha</term>, the Yogi should unite with me with composed mind." 8.

While practising, he should sit in the <term>Siddhasana: accomplished pose</term>, and having performed the internal locks and breath retention, should begin with 10 breath controls the first day, and go on increasing by 5 daily. 9.

With composed mind 80 retentions should be performed at a time; beginning first with the <term>moon: the left nostril</term> and then the <term>sun: the right nostril</term>. 10.

This has been spoken of by wise men as <term>Anuloma and Viloma: natural and regulated nostril breathing</term>. Having practised the Sun-Piercing breath, with the locks, the wise man should practise <term>Ujjayi: victorious breath</term> and then <term>Sitkari: hissing breath</term>, <term>Shitali: cooling breath</term>, and <term>Bhastrika: bellows breath</term>; he may practice others or not. 11-12.

He should practise the <term>Mudras: energetic seals or gestures</term> properly, as instructed by his teacher. Then sitting in the <term>Padmasana: lotus pose</term>, he should hear the <term>Anahata Nada: the "unstruck" internal sound</term> attentively. 13.

He should resign the fruits of all his practice reverently to God, and, on rising on the completion of the practice, a warm bath should be taken. 14.

The bath should bring all the daily duties briefly to an end. At noon also a little rest should be taken at the end of the exercise, and then food should be taken. 15.

Yogis should always take wholesome food and never anything unwholesome. After dinner he should eat <term>cardamom</term> or <term>cloves</term>. 16.

Some like camphor, and betel leaf. To the Yogis, practising breath control, betel leaf without powders, i.e., lime, nuts and <term>katha: catechu</term>, is beneficial. 17.

After taking food he should read books treating of salvation, or hear the ancient legends and repeat the name of God. 18.

In the evening, the exercise should be begun after finishing the <term>Sandhyâ</term> <note>ritual prayers and meditations performed at the "junctions" of the day, such as sunrise and sunset</note>, as before, beginning the practice three <term>Ghatikâs</term> <note>a traditional unit of time; 3 ghatikâs equal roughly 72 minutes</note> or one hour before the sun sets. 19.

Evening <term>Sandhyâ</term> should always be performed after practice, and <term>Hatha Yoga</term> should be practised at midnight. 20.

<term>Viparîta Karnî</term> <note>the "Inverted Action," a practice where the body is turned upside down</note> is to be practised in the evening and at midnight, and not just after eating, as it does no good at this time. 21.

### Now, Ujjâyî (The Victorious Breath)

->Closing the mouth, one should slowly draw in the breath through the throat,<-
->so that it is felt from the throat to the heart, making a resonant sound. 51.<-

### *Ujjâyî* (उज्जायी).

Having closed the opening of the <term>Nâdî</term> <note>here referring to the larynx or glottis</note>, the air should be drawn in such a way that it goes touching from the throat to the chest, and making noise while passing. 51.

->The breath should be restrained as before, and then exhaled through the Idâ.<-
->This removes phlegm-related disorders in the throat and increases the body's digestive fire. 52.<-

It should be restrained, as before, and then let out through <term>Idâ</term> <note>the left nostril, associated with the moon and cooling energy</note>. This removes <term>Śleṣmâ</term> <note>phlegm or mucus, one of the three traditional humors in Ayurvedic medicine</note> in the throat and increases the appetite. 52.

->It destroys water retention, disorders of the energy channels, and imbalances of the tissues.<-
->The breath control known as Ujjâyî should be performed whether walking or standing. 53.<-

It destroys the defects of the <term>Nâdîs</term> <note>the subtle channels through which life force or 'Prana' flows</note>, dropsy <note>edema or swelling caused by excess fluid</note> and disorders of <term>Dhâtu</term> <note>the seven fundamental tissues of the body: plasma, blood, muscle, fat, bone, marrow, and reproductive fluid</note>. Ujjâyî should be performed in all conditions of life, even while walking or sitting. 53.

### Now, Sîtkârî (The Hissing Breath)

->One should make a 'sit' sound in the mouth, and inhale with the nose...<-
->By this practice, one becomes like a second God of Love. 54.<-

### *Sîtkârî* (सीत्कारी).

Sîtkârî is performed by drawing in the air through the mouth, keeping the tongue between the lips <note>original Sanskrit suggests making a 'sit' hissing sound through the teeth or lips</note>. The air thus drawn in should not be expelled through the mouth. By practising in this way, one becomes next to the <term>God of Love</term> <note>Kâmadeva, the deity of desire and beauty</note> in beauty. 54.

->He is common to the circle of Yoginîs and can create or destroy.<-
->Neither hunger, nor thirst, nor sleep, nor laziness will arise for him. 55.<-

He is regarded adorable by the <term>Yoginîs</term> <note>enlightened female practitioners or semi-divine beings</note> and becomes the destroyer of the cycle of creation. He is not afflicted with hunger, thirst, sleep or lassitude. 55.

->The <term>Sattva</term> <note>the quality of purity, light, and harmony</note> of his body becomes free from all disturbances.<-

### Now, Sîtalî (The Cooling Breath)

->Drawing in the air with the tongue, perform breath retention as before.<-
->The wise practitioner should then slowly exhale the air through both nostrils. 57.<-

->*Sîtalî* <note>original: "शीतली" — a cooling pranayama technique</note><-

As in the technique described above <note>referring to Sitkari</note>, the tongue is to be protruded a little out of the lips while the air is drawn in. It is kept confined <note>held in the lungs</note>, as before, and then expelled slowly through the nostrils. 57.

->This <term>Sîtalî</term> practice cures diseases of the spleen and abdomen, fever,<-
->excess bile, hunger, and thirst; it even destroys the effects of poisons. 58.<-

This <term>Sîtalî Kumbhikâ</term> <note>breath retention involving the cooling breath</note> cures colic, an enlarged spleen, fever, disorders of the bile, hunger, thirst, and counteracts poisons. 58.

### Now, Bhastrikâ (The Bellows Breath)

->Placing both soles of the feet firmly upon the opposite thighs:<-
->this is <term>Padma Âsana</term>, the destroyer of all sins. 59.<-

->*The Bhastrikâ* <note>original: "भस्त्रिका" — named after the rapid movement of a blacksmith's bellows</note><-

The <term>Padma Âsana</term> <note>the Lotus Posture</note> consists in crossing the feet and placing them on both the thighs; it is the destroyer of all sins. 59.

->Firmly assuming the Lotus Posture, keeping the neck and abdomen straight,<-
->the wise one closes the mouth and exhales forcefully through the nose. 60.<-

Binding the <term>Padma-Âsana</term> and keeping the body straight, closing the mouth carefully, let the air be expelled through the nose. 60.

->So that it is felt in the heart, throat, and up to the skull, making a sound;<-
->one should also fill the "lotus of the heart" with air with great speed. 61.<-

It should be filled up to the lotus of the heart, by drawing it in with force, making noise and touching the throat, the chest and the head. 61.

->Expel it again and fill it again, repeatedly,<-
->just as a blacksmith works his bellows with speed. 62.<-

It should be expelled again and filled again and again as before, just as a pair of bellows of the blacksmith is worked. 62.

->In the same way, one should intelligently move the air within the body;<-
->whenever the body feels fatigued, one should inhale through the "Sun." 63.<-

In the same way, the air of the body should be moved intelligently, filling it through <term>Sûrya</term> <note>the right nostril, associated with the heating "Sun" channel</note> when fatigue is experienced. 63.

->Just as the belly becomes full of air, in that light manner,<-
->one should hold the nose firmly, without using the middle and index fingers. 64.<-

The air should be drawn in through the right nostril by pressing the thumb against the left side of the nose, so as to close the left nostril; and when filled to the full, it should be closed with the fourth finger (the one next to the little finger) and kept confined <note>this describes the Vishnu Mudra, where the index and middle fingers are folded down</note>. 64.

->Having performed the breath retention according to the rules, exhale the air through the "Moon."<-
->This destroys disorders of Wind, Bile, and Phlegm, and increases the body’s fire. 65.<-

Having confined it properly <note>performing Kumbhaka</note>, it should be expelled through the <term>Idâ</term> <note>the left nostril, associated with the cooling "Moon" channel</note>. This destroys <term>Vâta</term> <note>wind/air</note>, <term>Pitta</term> <note>bile</note> and <term>Kapha</term> <note>phlegm</note> and increases the digestive power (the gastric fire). 65.

->It quickly awakens the Kundalinî, provides comfort, and is beneficial.<-
->It destroys the blockages of phlegm located at the entrance of the Brahma Nâdî. 66.<-

It quickly awakens the <term>Kundalinî</term> <note>the dormant spiritual energy at the base of the spine</note>, purifies the system, gives pleasure, and is beneficial. It destroys phlegm and the impurities accumulated at the entrance of the <term>Brahma Nâdî</term> <note>the central spiritual channel, also called the Sushumna</note>. 66.

->It is capable of piercing the three knots that arise in the body.<-
->Therefore, this breath retention called Bhastrikâ should be specially practiced. 67.<-

This <term>Bhastrikâ</term> <note>literally "the bellows," named for the rapid movement of air</note> should be performed plentifully, for it breaks the three knots: <term>Brahma granthi</term> (in the chest), <term>Vishnu granthi</term> (in the throat), and <term>Rudra granthi</term> (between the eyebrows) of the body. 67.

### Now, Bhrâmari (The Humming Bee Breath)

->Fill the air with force, making the sound of a male bee, and exhale very slowly, making the sound of a female bee.<-
->Through this practice, a certain state of ecstatic play arises in the minds of the great Yogis. 68.<-

->The *Bhrâmari* <note>original: "भ्रामरी" — the Bee Breath</note><-

By filling the air with force, making a noise like <term>Bhringi</term> <note>a male bee or wasp</note>, and expelling it slowly, making noise in the same way; this practice causes a sort of ecstasy in the minds of the <term>Yogîndras</term> <note>the lords among practitioners of yoga</note>. 68.

### Now, Mûrchha (The Swooning Breath)

->At the end of inhalation, perform the chin-lock very firmly;<-
->then exhale slowly. This is known as Mûrchhana, which causes the mind to swoon and gives happiness. 69.<-

<note>The Mûrchha breath involves a prolonged retention that causes a pleasant, temporary suspension of conscious thought.</note>

### *The Mûrchhâ <note>literally "fainting" or "swooning"</note>.*

Closing the passages with <term>Jâlandhar Bandha</term> <note>the throat lock, where the chin is pressed against the chest</note> firmly at the end of <term>Pûraka</term> <note>the inhalation phase</note>, and expelling the air slowly, is called Mûrchhâ, from its causing the mind to swoon and giving comfort. 69.

->Now, the <term>Plâvinî</term> <note>the "floating" breath</note> technique:<-
->With the belly filled by a great volume of air moved within,<-
->one floats easily even on deep water, like a lotus leaf. 70.<-

### *The Plâvinî (The Floating Breath).*

When the belly is filled with air and the inside of the body is filled to its utmost with air, the body floats on the deepest water, like the leaf of a lotus. 70.

->Breath control is said to be of three types: exhalation, inhalation, and retention;<-
->Retention is considered to be of two types: Accompanied and Absolute. 71.<-

Considering <term>Pûraka</term> (Filling), <term>Rechaka</term> <note>original: "Rechaka"</note> (expelling) and <term>Kumbhaka</term> <note>original: "Kumbhaka"</note> (confining), <term>Prânâyâma</term> <note>breath control</note> is of three kinds, but considering it accompanied by Pûraka and Rechaka, and without these, it is of two kinds only, i.e., <term>Sahita</term> <note>literally "with" or "accompanied"</note> and <term>Kevala</term> <note>literally "alone" or "absolute"</note>. 71.

->One should practice the Accompanied technique until success in the Absolute is gained.<-
->The latter is simply holding the breath with ease, letting go of exhalation and inhalation. 72.<-

Exercise in Sahita should be continued till success in Kevala is gained. This latter is simply confining the air with ease, without Rechaka and Pûraka. 72.

->This breath control is called the Absolute Retention.<-
->When the Absolute Retention is perfected, devoid of exhalation and inhalation... 73.<-

In the practice of Kevala Prânâyâma when it can be performed successfully without Rechaka and Pûraka, then it is called <term>Kevala Kumbhaka</term> <note>the state of spontaneous breath suspension</note>. 73.

->Nothing in the three worlds is difficult for him to obtain,<-
->who is capable of holding the breath at will through Absolute Retention. 74.<-

There is nothing in the three worlds <note>the physical, astral, and causal realms</note> which may be difficult to obtain for him who is able to keep the air confined according to pleasure, by means of Kevala Kumbhaka. 74.

->He undoubtedly attains the state of Râja Yoga;<-
->from retention comes the awakening of Kundalinî, and from that awakening... 75.<-

He obtains the position of <term>Râja Yoga</term> <note>the "Royal Path," the highest stage of meditation</note> undoubtedly. <term>Kundalinî</term> <note>the dormant spiritual energy at the base of the spine</note>

<summary>This page describes the Mûrchhâ (swooning) and Plâvinî (floating) breathing techniques and introduces the distinction between Sahita (intentional) and Kevala (spontaneous) breath retention. It emphasizes that mastering spontaneous retention (Kevala Kumbhaka) leads to the awakening of Kundalinî and the attainment of Râja Yoga.</summary>

# THE HATHA YOGA PRADIPIKA.

<meta>Institutional stamps from the Ministry of Culture and the Central Secretariat Library are present at the top corners of the original document.</meta>

The <term>Kuṇḍalinī</term> <note>the dormant spiritual energy at the base of the spine</note> awakens by <term>Kumbhaka</term> <note>breath retention</note>, and by its awakening, the <term>Suṣumnâ</term> <note>the central energy channel of the subtle body</note> becomes free from impurities. 75.

->The Suṣumnâ becomes unobstructed and success in Haṭha Yoga is born.<-
->Without Haṭha Yoga, there is no Râja Yoga; without Râja Yoga, there is no Haṭha Yoga.<-
->Therefore, one should practice the pair together until perfection is reached. 76.<-

No success in <term>Râja Yoga</term> <note>the "royal" path focusing on the mind and meditation</note> without <term>Haṭha Yoga</term> <note>the "forceful" path focusing on the body and breath</note>, and no success in Haṭha Yoga without Râja Yoga. One should, therefore, practise both of these well, till complete success is gained. 76.

->At the end of the breath retention in Kumbhaka, one should make the mind supportless.<-
->Through this practice of yoga, one attains the state of Râja Yoga. 77.<-

On the completion of Kumbhaka, the mind should be given rest <note>original Sanskrit: <insert>nirāśrayam</insert>, meaning "without support" or "objectless"</note>. By practising in this way one is raised to the position of (succeeds in getting) Râja Yoga. 77.

->Slimness of body, a joyful face, clarity of the inner sound, very clear eyes,<-
->freedom from disease, control of the vital essence, stimulation of the digestive fire,<-
->and purification of the energy channels are the signs of success in Haṭha Yoga. 78.<-

->*Indications of success in the practice of Haṭha Yoga.*<-

When the body becomes lean, the face glows with delight, <term>Anâhata-nâda</term> <note>the "unstruck sound," a mystical internal resonance</note> manifests, and eyes are clear, body is healthy, <term>bindu</term> <note>the vital essence or life force</note> is under control, and appetite increases <note>literally "the kindling of the digestive fire"</note>, then one should know that the <term>Nâdîs</term> <note>the subtle energy channels</note> are purified and success in Haṭha Yoga is approaching. 78.

->End of Chapter II.<-

->Thus ends the second chapter of the <term>Haṭhapradīpikā</term> <note>literally "Light on Haṭha Yoga"</note>. 2.<-

# CHAPTER III.
### *On Mudrâs.*

## THE THIRD INSTRUCTION

->Just as the King of Serpents <note>original: *ahināyakaḥ*, referring to the mythical serpent Śeṣa who supports the world</note> is the support of the earth, with all its mountains and forests,<-
->so is the <term>Kuṇḍalinī</term> <note>the "coiled" spiritual energy dormant at the base of the spine</note> the fundamental support of all yogic practices. 1.<-

As the chief of the snakes is the support of the earth with all the mountains and forests on it, so all the <term>Tantras</term> <note>here referring to the practical systems or techniques of Yoga</note> rest on the Kuṇḍalinī. (The energy at the base of the vertebral column.) 1.

->When the sleeping Kuṇḍalinī is awakened through the grace of a <term>Guru</term> <note>a spiritual teacher or guide</note>,<-
->then all the "lotuses" and the "knots" are pierced through. 2.<-

When the sleeping Kuṇḍalinī awakens by favour of a guru, then all the lotuses (in the six <term>Chakras</term> <note>the subtle energy centers of the body</note>) and all the <term>Granthis</term> <note>the three "knots" or energy blockages along the spine</note> are pierced through. 2.

->The "Path of Emptiness" <note>the central energy channel</note> then becomes a royal highway for the life-breath;<-
->then the mind becomes free from support, and Death is evaded. 3.<-

The <term>Suṣumnâ</term> <note>the central channel of the subtle body</note> becomes a main road for the passage of <term>Prâṇa</term> <note>vital life force or breath</note>, and the mind then becomes free from all connections (with its objects of enjoyments) and Death is then evaded. 3.

->The Central Channel, the Path of Emptiness, the Opening of God, the Great Path,<-
->the Cremation Ground, the Way of Bliss, and the Middle Way are all synonyms. 4.<-

Suṣumnâ, Śūnya Padavî, Brahma Randhra, Mahâ Patha, Śmaśâna <note>literally "Cremation Ground," a metaphor for the place where the ego is destroyed</note>, Śâmbhavî, Madhya Mârga, are names of one and the same thing. 4.

->Therefore, to awaken this Goddess who is sleeping at the entrance of the Gate of God,<-
->one should diligently practice the <term>Mudrâs</term> <note>ritual gestures or physical seals used to direct energy</note>. 5.<-

In order, therefore, to awaken this goddess, who is sleeping at the entrance of Brahma Dwâra (the great door), mudrâs should be practised well. 5.

->Now, the different types of Mudrâs:<-
->The Great Seal, the Great Lock, the Great Piercing, and the Sky-Walker;<-
->the Upward-Flying Lock, the Root Lock, and the lock known as Jalandhara. 6.<-
*The mudrâs.*

<term>Mahâ Mudrâ</term> (The Great Seal), <term>Mahâ Bandha</term> (The Great Lock), <term>Mahâ Vedha</term> (The Great Piercing), <term>Khecharî</term> (The Sky-Walker), <term>Uḍḍiyâna Bandha</term> (The Upward Lock), <term>Mûla Bandha</term> (The Root Lock), <term>Jâlandhara Bandha</term> (The Throat Lock). 6.

->The Inverted Action, the Thunderbolt Act, and the Stirring of the Power.<-
->These are the ten Gestures which annihilate old age and death. 7.<-

<term>Viparîta Karanî</term> <note>the "Inverted Action," often identified with headstands or shoulder stands</note>, <term>Vajroli</term> <note>the "Thunderbolt Act," a practice involving the pelvic muscles</note>, and <term>Śakti Châlana</term> <note>the "Stirring of the Power," a technique to move the internal energy</note>. These are the ten Gestures which annihilate old age and death. 7.

->They were taught by the First Lord and bestow the eight divine perfections.<-
->They are cherished by all the Perfected Masters and are difficult even for the Gods of the Wind to master. 8.<-

They have been explained by <term>Âdi Nâtha</term> <note>the "First Lord," an epithet for the god Śiva, the legendary founder of Yoga</note> and give eight kinds of divine wealth <note>the *Siddhis* or supernatural powers</note>. They are loved by all the <term>Siddhas</term> <note>Perfected Masters who have attained spiritual completion</note> and are hard to attain even by the <term>Marutas</term> <note>the Vedic storm deities or gods of the wind</note>. 8.

<note>The eight Divine Perfections (Aiśwaryas) mentioned here are: 
1. **Animâ**: The ability to become as small as an atom.
2. **Mahimâ**: The ability to become vast like the sky by expanding one's essence.
3. **Garimâ**: The ability to become incredibly heavy, like a mountain.
4. **Prâpti**: The power of reach, such as touching the moon with a finger while standing on earth.
5. **Prâkâmya**: Irresistible will, such as moving through solid ground as if it were water.
6. **Îsatâ**: Absolute mastery over matter and its forms.
7. **Vasitwa**: The power to control both animate and inanimate objects.</note>

->These should be kept secret with great effort, like a box of precious jewels.<-
->They must never be spoken of to anyone, just as a noblewoman keeps her intimacy private. 9.<-

These Gestures should be kept secret by every means, as one keeps one's box of jewellery, and should, on no account be told to any one, just as husband and wife keep their dealings secret. 9.

### The Great Gesture (Mahâ Mudrâ)

->Pressing the perineum with the left heel, and extending the right leg,<-
->one should firmly grasp the foot with both hands. 10.<-

->*The Great Gesture (Mahâ Mudrâ)*<-

Pressing the <term>Yoni</term> <note>here referring specifically to the perineum, the space between the anus and genitals</note> with the heel of the left foot, and stretching forth the right foot, its toe should be grasped by the thumb and first finger. 10.

->Applying the throat-lock, one should hold the breath in the upper channels.<-
->Just as a snake struck with a rod becomes straight like a staff, 11.<-
->So the Energy suddenly becomes straight.<-
->Then the state of death comes to the two side-channels. 12.<-

By stopping the throat (by applying the <term>Jâlandhara Bandha</term> <note>the Chin Lock, where the chin is pressed against the chest to seal energy</note>) the air is drawn in from the outside and carried down. Just as a snake struck with a stick becomes straight like a stick, in the same way, the <term>Śakti</term> <note>the Kuṇḍalinī energy</note> becomes straight at once within the central channel. Then the <term>Kuṇḍalinī</term> enters a state where the life-breath no longer flows through the two side-channels <note>the Iḍā and Piṅgalā</note>, and they become as if dead. 12.

...leaving both the <term>Idâ</term> <note>the left energy channel associated with the moon</note> and the <term>Pingalâ</term> <note>the right energy channel associated with the sun</note>, the breath enters the <term>suṣumnâ</term> <note>the central channel of the subtle body</note>. 11-12.

The breath should then be expelled slowly, never with sudden force. For this reason, the most excellent of the wise call this the <term>Mahâ Mudrâ</term> <note>The Great Gesture</note>. This Great Gesture has been demonstrated by the <term>Mahâsiddhas</term> <note>Great Masters of yoga who have attained perfection</note>. 13.

Great afflictions and flaws, including death itself, are diminished by it; for this very reason, the most excellent of the wise call it the Great Gesture. 14.

Having practiced with the <term>moon-limb</term> <note>original: *candrâṅge*; referring to the left nostril or side of the body</note>, one should then practice with the <term>sun-limb</term> <note>original: *sūryāṅge*; referring to the right nostril or side</note>. When the number of repetitions on both sides becomes equal, then the gesture should be discontinued. 15.

There is no longer a distinction between wholesome and unwholesome food; all tastes—even those that are flavorless—become beneficial. Even the most terrible of poisons, if consumed, is digested as if it were <term>nectar</term> <note>original: *pīyūṣam*; the divine ambrosia that grants immortality</note>. 16.

Wasting diseases <note>consumption/tuberculosis</note>, skin ailments <note>leprosy</note>, chronic constipation, abdominal tumors, and disorders arising from indigestion—all these maladies are destroyed for the one who practices the Great Gesture. 17.

This Great Gesture has been described as the giver of <term>Great Success</term> <note>original: *Mahâsiddhi*; the attainment of supreme spiritual powers</note> to men. It must be kept secret with great effort and should not be revealed to just anyone. 18.

-> Now, the Great Lock <-
<note>original: *Atha Mahâbandhaḥ*</note>

Place the heel of the left foot at the <term>perineum</term> <note>original: *yonisthāne*; the "place of the source," the space between the genitals and the anus</note>. Then, place the right foot firmly upon the left thigh. 19.

# THE HATHA YOGA PRADIPIKA.

->*The Great Bond.*<-
<note>original: "The Mahâ Bandha"</note>

Press the left heel to the <term>perineum</term> <note>the area between the anus and genitals</note> and place the right foot on the left thigh. 19.

->Having inhaled the breath, hold the chin firmly against the chest.<-
<note>original: "pūrayitvā tato vāyuṃ hṛdaye cubukaṃ dṛḍham"</note>
->Having compressed the air and contracted the muscles, the mind should be fixed on the space between the eyebrows or within the central channel.<-
<note>original: "niṣpīḍya vāyumākuñcya manomadhye niyojayet"; the "central channel" refers to the suṣumnâ in the spine. 20.</note>

Having kept the breath confined for as long as possible, it should be expelled slowly. Having practiced this on the left side of the body, it should then be practiced on the right side. 21.

->Some are of the opinion that the closing of the throat is not necessary here,<-
->claiming that a lock formed by the tongue against the roots of the upper teeth is effective.<-
<note>original: "matamatra tu keṣāñcitkaṇṭhabandhaṃ vivarjayet...". 22.</note>

This practice stops the upward motion of energy in all the <term>Nâḍîs</term> <note>the subtle channels of the body</note>. Truly, this <term>Great Bond</term> <note>Mahâ Bandha</note> is the giver of great <term>Siddhis</term> <note>spiritual perfections or extraordinary powers</note>. 23.

->This Great Bond is the most skillful means for cutting away the snares of death.<-
->It brings about the meeting of the three channels and carries the mind to the seat of Śiva.<-
<note>original: "kālapāśamahābandhavimocanavicakṣaṇaḥ..."; the "meeting of three channels" refers to the Triveṇî (Iḍâ, Piṅgalâ, and Suṣumnâ) and "the seat of Śiva" refers to Kedâr, the space between the eyebrows. 24.</note>

->Just as a woman endowed with beauty and loveliness is without fruit without a husband,<-
->so the Great Gesture and the Great Bond are useless without the Great Piercing.<-
<note>original: "rūpalāvaṇyasaṃpannā yathā strī puruṣaṃ vinā..."; refers to Mahâ Mudrâ, Mahâ Bandha, and Mahâ Vedha. 25.</note>

### Now, the Great Piercing.
<note>original: "Atha Mahâvedhaḥ"</note>

->The yogi, remaining in the Great Bond, should perform an inhalation with a single-minded focus.<-
->He should arrest the movement of the breaths steadily by using the throat-seal.<-
<note>original: "mahābandhasthito yogī kṛtvā pūrakamekadhīḥ..."; the throat-seal is the kaṇṭha-mudrâ. 26.</note>

### *The Great Penetration*
<note>original: "The Mahâ Vedha"</note>

Sitting in the posture of the Great Bond, the practitioner should inhale and keep the mind focused. The movements of the <term>Vâyus</term> <note>the internal "winds" or life-forces</note> known as <term>Prâna</term> and <term>Apâna</term> should be halted by closing the throat. 26.

->Placing both hands equally on the ground, one should raise the body slightly and strike the buttocks against the ground gently.<-
<note>original: "samahastayugo bhūmau sphicau saṃtāḍayecchanaiḥ"</note>
->Leaving the two side channels, the breath begins to vibrate within the central path.<-
<note>The air leaves the <term>Idâ</term> and <term>Pingalâ</term> channels to enter the <term>Suṣumnâ</term> or central channel of the spine. 27.</note>

->A union of the lunar, solar, and fire energies is formed for the sake of immortality.<-
<note>original: "somasūryāgnisaṃbandho jāyate cāmṛtāya vai"; referring to the meeting of energies from the left and right channels within the central channel.</note>
->When the air reaches a state of stillness—becoming as if dead—it should then be exhaled.<- 28.

->This Great Penetration, through consistent practice, is the giver of great accomplishments.<-
<note>original: "mahāvedho'yamabhyāsānmahāsiddhipradāyakaḥ"; <term>Siddhis</term> are extraordinary spiritual or physical powers.</note>
->It destroys wrinkles, grey hair, and the shaking of the body, and is therefore practiced by the greatest of masters.<- 29.

->These <term>THREE</term> are the great secrets, the destroyers of old age and death.<-
<note>The "three" refers to the Mahâ Mudrâ, Mahâ Bandha, and Mahâ Vedha described in this section.</note>
->They increase the digestive fire and grant perfections such as the power to become as small as an atom.<-
<note>original: "vahnivṛddhikaraṃ caiva hyaṇimādiguṇapradam"; <term>Animâ</term> is the first of the eight classic yogic powers. 30.</note>

->They should be performed eight times daily, at every three-hour interval.<-
->This practice accumulates merit and constantly dissolves the stream of past sins.<-
->For those well-instructed, the initial practice should be done in small measures.<- 31.

### The Sky-Walking Gesture
<note>original: "Khecharî Mudrâ"</note>

->When the tongue is turned backward and enters the hollow of the skull,<-
->and the gaze is fixed between the eyebrows, this is the Sky-Walking Gesture.<-
<note>original: "kapālakuhare jihvā praviṣṭā viparītagā...". 32.</note>

# THE HATHA YOGA PRADIPIKA.

F

### *The Sky-Walking Gesture*
<note>original: "The Khechari"</note>

The Sky-Walking Gesture <term>Khechari Mudrâ</term> <note>literally "moving in the void or sky"</note> is accomplished by thrusting the tongue into the gullet <note>the hollow behind the palate</note>, by turning it back upon itself, and fixing the gaze upon the point between the eyebrows. 32.

->By cutting, moving, and stretching, one should gradually increase the length of the tongue.<-
->When it can touch the space between the eyebrows, then the Sky-Walking Gesture is perfected. 33.<-
<note>original: "chedanacālanadohaiḥ kalāṃ krameṇa vardhayettāvat || sā yāvadbhrūmadhyaṃ spṛśati tadā khecarisiddhiḥ ||"</note>

To accomplish this, the tongue is lengthened by cutting the <term>frænum linguæ</term> <note>the small fold of mucous membrane extending from the floor of the mouth to the midline of the underside of the tongue</note>, moving, and pulling it. When it can touch the space between the eyebrows, then Khechari can be accomplished. 33.

->Taking a sharp, smooth, and clean instrument, shaped like a cactus leaf,<-
->one should cut the membrane a hair’s breadth at a time. 34.<-
<note>original: "snuhīpatranibhaṃ śastraṃ sutīkṣṇaṃ snigdhanirmalam || samādāya tatastena romamātraṃ samucchinet ||"</note>

Taking a sharp, smooth, and clean instrument, of the shape of a cactus leaf, the frænum of the tongue should be cut a little (as much as a hair's thickness) at a time. 34.

->Then rock salt and yellow myrobalan, both powdered, should be applied.<-
->After seven days have passed, one should again cut it a hair’s breadth. 35.<-
<note>original: "tataḥ saiṃdhavapathyābhyāṃ cūrṇitābhyāṃ prakarṣayet || punaḥ saptadine prāpte romamātraṃ samucchinet ||"; Myrobalan is a medicinal fruit used in Ayurveda for healing.</note>

Then rock salt and yellow myrobalan (both powdered) should be rubbed in. On the 7th day, it should again be cut a hair's breadth. 35.

->One should continue this practice regularly for six months.<-
->After six months, the bond at the root of the tongue is destroyed. 36.<-
<note>original: "evaṃ krameṇa ṣaṇmāsaṃ nityaṃ yuktaḥ samācaret || ṣaṇmāsādrasanāmūlaśilābandhaḥ praṇaśyati ||"</note>

One should go on doing thus, regularly for six months. At the end of six months, the frænum of the tongue will be completely cut <note>the "bond" or "stone-like knot" at the root of the tongue is severed</note>. 36.

->Turning the tongue upward and back, one should join it to the "triple path."<-
->This becomes the Sky-Walking Gesture, and is called the Wheel of the Void. 37.<-
<note>original: "kalāṃ parāṅmukhīṃ kṛtvā tripathe pariyojayet || sā bhavetkhecarī mudrā vyomacakraṃ taducyate ||"; The "triple path" refers to the junction where the nasal and oral passages meet.</note>

Turning the tongue upwards, it is fixed on the three ways (œsophagus, windpipe and palate). Thus it makes the Khechari Mudrâ, and is called the <term>Vyoma Chakra</term> <note>the Wheel of the Void or Sky</note>. 37.

->If the tongue remains turned upward even for half a moment,<-
->the practitioner is freed from poisons, diseases, death, and old age. 38.<-
<note>original: "rasanā mūrdhvagāṃ kṛtvā kṣaṇārdhamapi tiṣṭhati || viṣairvimumucyate yogī vyādhimṛtyujarādhibhiḥ ||"</note>

The <term>Yogî</term> who sits for a minute turning his tongue upwards, is saved from poisons, diseases, death, old age, etc. 38.

->He who knows the Sky-Walking Gesture is not afflicted<-
->by disease, death, lethargy, sleep, hunger, thirst, or fainting. 39.<-
<note>original: "na rogo maraṇaṃ taṃdrā na nidrā na kṣudhā tṛṣā || na ca mūrchā bhavettasya yo mudrāṃ vetti khecarīm ||"</note>

He who knows the Khechari Mudrâ is not afflicted with disease, death, sloth, sleep, hunger, thirst, and swooning. 39.

<summary>This page describes the "Khechari Mudra" (Sky-Walking Gesture), a practice involving the surgical lengthening of the tongue's frenum over six months. Once the tongue can reach the nasal cavity, it is said to grant the Yogi immunity to disease, aging, and physical needs like hunger and thirst.</summary>
<keywords>Khechari Mudra, tongue lengthening, frenum linguæ, Vyoma Chakra, Hatha Yoga, immortality, Ayurvedic medicine, yellow myrobalan</keywords>

# THE HATHA YOGA PRADIPIKA.

->One who knows the Sky-Walking Gesture <term>Khechari Mudrâ</term> is not troubled by disease, is not stained by the consequences of his actions <term>karmas</term>, and is not caught in the snare of time. 40.<-
<note>original: "pīḍyate na sa rogeṇa lipyate na ca karmaṇā || bādhyate na sa kālena yo mudrāṃ vetti khecarīm || 40 ||"</note>

->The perfected masters <term>Siddhas</term> have named this the "Sky-Walking Gesture" because the mind and the tongue reach the inner void <term>âkâśa</term> <note>literally "sky" or "ether," referring here to the space behind the palate</note> through its practice. 41.<-
<note>original: "cittaṃ carati khe yasmājjihvā carati khe gatā || tenaiṣā khecarī nāma mudrā siddhairnirūpitā || 41 ||"</note>

->If the opening behind the palate is sealed using the Sky-Walking Gesture by turning the tongue upwards, then the vital essence <term>bindu</term> <note>the life-force or "seed" of the body</note> cannot leave its place, even if one were in the embrace of a woman. 42.<-
<note>original: "khecaryā mudritaṃ yena vivaraṃ lambikordhvataḥ || na tasya kṣarate binduḥ kāminyāśleṣitasyaca || 42 ||"</note>

->If the practitioner of Yoga drinks the Moon-nectar <term>Somarasa</term> <note>a divine juice believed to flow from the head</note> by sitting with the tongue turned backwards and the mind concentrated, there is no doubt they shall conquer death within fifteen days. 43.<-
<note>original: "ūrdhvajihvaḥ sthiro bhūtvā somapānaṃ karoti yaḥ || māsārdhena na saṃdeho mṛtyuṃ jayati yogavit || 43 ||"</note>

->If the Yogi, whose body is full of this Moon-nectar, were bitten by the king of serpents <term>Takshaka</term>, the poison could not spread through his body. 44.<-
<note>original: "nityaṃ somakalāpūrṇaṃ śarīre yasya yoginaḥ || takṣakeṇāpi daṣṭasya viṣaṃ tasya na sarpati || 44 ||"</note>

->Just as fire is inseparable from wood, and a lamp is inseparable from its wick and oil, so the soul does not leave a body that is filled with the nectar flowing from the Moon <term>Soma</term>. 45.<-
<note>original: "indhanāni yathā vahnistailavarttiṃ ca dīpakaḥ || tathā somakalāpūrṇo dehī dehaṃ na muṃcati || 45 ||"</note>

Note.—The Moon <term>Soma</term> or <term>Chandra</term> is described later in this text as being located in the "thousand-petalled lotus" <note>the Sahasrara or crown center</note> in the human brain. It is the same moon seen on the head of the god <term>Śiva</term> in traditional paintings, from which a divine juice exudes. Retaining this fluid is what grants the practitioner immortality.

->One should daily "eat the flesh of the cow" and "drink the immortal liquor." I consider such a person to be of noble family <term>Kulina</term>; others are merely destroyers of their families. 46.<-
<note>This is a coded, esoteric instruction. In Hatha Yoga, "cow" refers to the tongue, and "eating its flesh" means inserting it into the throat cavity. The "immortal liquor" is the nectar produced during the practice, not actual alcohol.</note>

<summary>This page describes the spiritual and physical benefits of Khechari Mudra, claiming it grants immunity to disease, karma, and death. It explains the "Moon-nectar" (Somarasa) that practitioners aim to retain to achieve a state of immortality and physical resilience.</summary>

<note>The following sections are commentary and scriptural citations provided by the editor to support the main text.</note>

**Note.**—The parents are fulfilled, the land is blessed, and that family is fortunate where a practitioner of Yoga is born; any gift given to such a person becomes inexhaustible. One who can distinguish between the pure Consciousness <term>Puruṣa</term> and the material nature <term>Prakṛiti</term> purifies the sins of a million incarnations simply by being seen, spoken to, or touched.
<note>original: "कृतार्थौ पितरौ तेन धन्यो देशः कुलं च तत् । जायते योगवान्यत्र दत्तंमक्षयतां व्रजेत् ॥ दृष्टः संभाषितः स्पृष्टः पुंप्रकृत्या विवेकवान् । भवकोटि शतापातं पुनाति वृजिनं नृणाम् ॥"</note>
->From the *Brahmavaivarta Purana*<-

A practitioner of Yoga far exceeds a thousand householders, a hundred forest-dwelling hermits <term>vânaprasthas</term>, and a thousand celibate students <term>Brahmacharîs</term>.
<note>original: "गृहस्थानां सहस्रेण वानप्रस्थशतेन च । ब्रह्मचारिसहस्रेण योगाभ्यासी विशिष्यते ॥"</note>
->From the *Brahmanda Purana*<-

Who can truly know the greatness of the Royal Yoga <term>Râja Yoga</term>? The country where a knower of this truth resides becomes a vessel of merit. By seeing and honoring such a person, twenty-one generations of one's family, even if ignorant, reach the state of liberation <term>mokṣa</term>; how much more so for those who are devoted to the practice themselves! He who knows both the internal and external Yoga is worthy of adoration by you and me <note>referring to the divine figures speaking in the Purana</note>; how much more so by the rest of mankind!

Those who engage in the Great Yoga <term>Mahâyoga</term> once, twice, or thrice daily, or even constantly, are to be recognized as Great Lords <term>Maheshwaras</term> or masters of supreme wealth.
<note>original: "राजयोग, कूर्मपुराणे" — From the *Kurma Purana* on Raja Yoga. "एक कालं... विज्ञे यास्ते महेश्वराः"</note>

->***<-

The word *Go* <term>cow</term> signifies the tongue; thrusting it into the palate is known as "eating the flesh of the cow." This practice destroys the most terrible sins. 47.
<note>original: "गोशब्देनोदिता जिह्वा तत्प्रवेशो हि तालुनि ॥ गोमांसभक्षणं तत्त महापातकनाशनम् ॥ ४७ ॥" This is an esoteric pun; "Go" in Sanskrit means both "cow" and "tongue."</note>

The immortal liquor <term>amaravâruṇî</term> is the essence that flows from the Moon <note>the energy center located in the head</note>. It is produced by the internal fire generated when the tongue is thrust upward into the cavity. 48.
<note>original: "जिह्वाप्रवेशसंभूतवह्निनात्पादितः खलु ॥ चंद्रात्स्रवति यः सारः स स्यादमरवारुणी ॥ ४८ ॥"</note>

If the tongue can constantly touch the opening with its tip, the fluid <term>rasa</term> begins to flow. This juice may taste salty, bitter, sour, or like milk, honey, or clarified butter <term>ghee</term>. Through this, one gains the power to destroy diseases, halt the aging process, and withstand the strike of weapons. Such a person achieves immortality, the eight supernatural powers, and the ability to attract celestial maidens <term>Siddhânganâs</term>. 49.
<note>original: "चुंबंती यदि लंबिकाग्रमनिशं जिहारसस्पंदिनी... सिद्धांगनाकर्षणम् ॥ ४९ ॥"</note>

...honey, one can drive away disease, destroy old age, can evade an attack of arms, become immortal in eight ways and can attract fairies. 49.

-><note>original: "मूर्ध्नः षोडशपत्रपद्मगलितं प्राणादवाप्तं हठादूर्ध्वास्यो रसनां नियम्य विवरे शक्तिं परां चिन्तयन् ॥ उत्कल्लोलकलाजलं च विमलं धारामयं यः पिबेन्निर्व्याधिः स मृणालकोमलपुपुर्योगी चिरं जीवति ॥ ५० ॥"</note>
One who drinks the clear, pure stream of nectar trickling from the sixteen-petalled lotus in the head—attained through the control of <term>Prâna</term> (life-breath) and by turning the tongue upward into the hollow of the palate while meditating on the Supreme Power (<term>Kundalinî</term>)—becomes free from disease. Their body becomes as soft and graceful as a lotus stalk, and the practitioner of Yoga lives for a very long time. 50.<-

He who drinks the clear stream of liquor of the moon (<term>soma</term>) falling from the brain to the sixteen-petalled lotus <note>The sixteen-petalled lotus is identified as the Vishuddha Chakra, located at the throat, which acts as a collection point for the nectar.</note> (in the heart), obtained by means of <term>Prâna</term>, by applying the tongue to the hole of the pendant in the palate, and by meditating on the great power (<term>Kundalinî</term>), becomes free from disease and tender in body, like the stalk of a lotus, and the Yogî lives a very long life. 50.

-><note>original: "यत्प्रालेयं प्रहितसुषिरं मेरुमूर्ध्नि तरस्थं ॥ तस्मिंस्तत्त्वं प्रवदति सुधीस्तन्मुखं निम्नगानाम् ॥ चंद्रात्सारः स्रवति वपुषस्तेन मृत्युर्नराणां ॥ तद्वध्नीयात्सुकरणमथो नान्यथा कार्यसिद्धिः ॥ ५१ ॥"</note>
At the summit of the <term>Merû</term> (the spinal column) is a hidden opening. The wise say the ultimate Truth resides there; it is the source of the rivers. From the "Moon" in the head, the essence of the body flows downward, and it is through this loss of essence that humans face death. Therefore, one must bind or capture this flow through the proper practice; there is no other way to achieve success. 51.<-

On the top of the <term>Merû</term> (vertebral column), concealed in a hole, is the <term>Somarasa</term> (the nectar of the Moon); the wise, whose intellect is not overpowered by the qualities of <term>Rajas</term> (passion) and <term>Tamas</term> (inertia), but in whom <term>Sattva</term> (purity/clarity) is predominant, say there is the universal spirit (<term>âtma</term>) in it. It is the source of the three downward-flowing energy channels: the <term>Idâ</term>, <term>Pingalâ</term>, and <term>Susumnâ</term> <term>Nâdîs</term>, which are symbolically called the Ganges, the Yamuna, and the Sarasvati rivers. From that "Moon" is shed the essence of the body which causes the death of men. It should, therefore, be stopped from shedding. This Sky-Walking Gesture (<term>Khecharî Mudrâ</term>) is a very good instrument for this purpose. There is no other means of achieving this end. 51.

-><note>original: "सुषिरं ज्ञानजनकं पंचस्रोतः समन्वितम् ॥ तिष्ठते खेचरी मुद्रा तस्मिन् शून्ये निरंजने ॥ ५२ ॥"</note>
This opening is the generator of knowledge and is connected to five energy streams. In that pure, colorless void, the Sky-Walking Gesture should be established. 52.<-

This hole is the generator of knowledge and is the source of the five streams (<term>Idâ</term>, <term>Pingalâ</term>, etc.). In that colorless vacuum, <term>Khecharî Mudrâ</term> should be established. 52.

-><note>original: "एकं सृष्टिमयं बीजमेका मुद्रा च खेचरी ॥ एको देवो निरालंब एकावस्था मनोन्मनी ॥ ५३ ॥"</note>
There is only one "seed" from which the entire universe germinates; there is only one Gesture, known as the Sky-Walking Gesture (<term>Khecharî</term>). There is only one God, who is independent of all support, and there is only one supreme state, called the State Beyond Mind (<term>Manonmanî</term>). 53.<-

There is only one seed germinating the whole universe from it; and there is only one <term>Mudrâ</term>, called <term>Khecharî</term>. There is only one <term>deva</term> (god) without any one's support, and there is one condition called <term>Manonmanî</term>. 53.

### Now, the Abdominal Lock (Uḍḍiyâna Bandha)

> <note>original: "बद्धो येन सुषुम्नायां प्राणस्तूड्डीयते यतः । तस्मादुड्डीयनाख्योऽयं योगिभिः समुदाहृतः ॥ ५४ ॥"</note>

->The Abdominal Lock (Uḍḍiyâna Bandha).<-

The Yogis call this practice <term>Uḍḍiyâna</term> (the "Flying Up" lock) because, through its application, the <term>Prâna</term> <note>The vital life-force or breath.</note> (also called <term>Vâyu</term>) is made to fly or flow into the <term>Suṣumnâ</term> <note>The central spiritual channel of the subtle body, located along the spine.</note>. 54.

> <note>original: "उड्डीनं कुरुते यस्मादविश्रांतं महाखगः ॥ उड्डीयानं तदेव स्यात्तत्र बंधोऽभिधीयते ॥ ५५ ॥"</note>

It is called the "Flying Up" lock because the "Great Bird"—the life-force—flies through it without becoming exhausted. Its specific technique is explained below. 55.

> <note>original: "उदरे पश्चिमं तानं नाभेरुर्ध्वं च कारयेत् ॥ उड्डीयानो ह्यसौ बंधो मृत्युमातंगकेसरी ॥ ५६ ॥"</note>

The practitioner should pull the abdomen above the navel backward toward the spine. This Abdominal Lock acts like a lion against the elephant of death <note>A traditional metaphor suggesting the practice is a powerful defense against mortality.</note>. 56.

> <note>original: "उड्डियानं तु सहजं गुरुणा कथितं सदा ॥ अभ्यसेत्सततं यस्तु वृद्धोऽपि तरुणायते ॥ ५७ ॥"</note>

When learned directly from a teacher (<term>guru</term>), the Abdominal Lock is always easy to perform. One who practices it constantly, even if they are old, becomes young again. 57.

> <note>original: "नाभेरुर्ध्वमधश्चापि तानं कुर्यात्प्रयत्नतः ॥ षण्मासमभ्यसेन्मृत्युं जयत्येव न संशयः ॥ ५८ ॥"</note>

One should exert effort to draw the areas both above and below the navel backward toward the spine. By practicing this for six months, one can undoubtedly conquer death. 58.

> <note>original: "सर्वेषामेव बंधानामुत्तमो ह्युड्डियानकः ॥ उड्डियाने दृढे बंधे मुक्तिः स्वाभाविकी भवेत् ॥ ५९ ॥"</note>

Of all the various locks (<term>bandhas</term>), the Abdominal Lock is the most superior. When this lock is firmly mastered, liberation (<term>mukti</term>) follows naturally. 59.

### Now, the Root Lock (Mûla Bandha)

> <note>original: "पार्ष्णिभागेन संपीड्य योनिमाकुंचयेद्गुदम् । अपानमूर्ध्वमाकृष्य मूलबंधोऽभिधीयते ॥ ६० ॥"</note>

->The Root Lock (Mûla Bandha).<-

Press the <term>yoni</term> <note>The perineum, the space between the genitals and the anus.</note> with the heel and contract the anus. By drawing the <term>apâna</term> <note>The downward-moving energy responsible for elimination.</note> upward in this way, the Root Lock is performed. 60.

<keywords>Uḍḍiyâna Bandha, Abdominal Lock, Mûla Bandha, Root Lock, Prâna, Suṣumnâ, Apâna, rejuvenation, conquest of death, Hatha Yoga</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the technical instructions on yogic "locks" (bandhas). It transitions from the conclusion of the Abdominal Lock (Uḍḍiyâna Bandha) to a detailed explanation of the Root Lock (Mûla Bandha) and its effects on the body's internal fire and the Kundalinî energy.</meta>

# THE LIGHT ON HATHA YOGA (HATHA YOGA PRADIPIKA).

<meta>Institutional stamps from the Government of India and the Central Secretariat Library are visible at the top corners of the original page.</meta>

->By force, one makes the downward-moving breath (<term>Apâna</term>) go upward<-
->by contracting the perineum; Yogis call this the Root Lock (<term>Mûla Bandha</term>). 61.<-
<note>original: "अधोगतिमपानं वा ऊर्ध्वं कुरुते बलात् । आकुंचनेन तं प्राहुर्मूलबंधं हि योगिनः ॥ ६१ ॥"</note>

The <term>Apâna</term> <note>The vital energy responsible for downward movement and elimination.</note>, which naturally inclining downward, is made to go up by force. This <term>Mûla Bandha</term> <note>The "Root Lock," performed by contracting the muscles of the pelvic floor.</note> is spoken of by Yogis as being performed by contracting the anus. 61.

->Pressing the heel firmly against the perineum, draw up the air by force<-
->repeatedly, until the vital breaths travel upward. 62.<-
<note>original: "गुदं पाष्ण्या तु संपीड्य वायुमाकुंचयेद् बलात् ॥ वारंवारं यथा चोर्ध्वं समायाति समीरणाः ॥ ६२ ॥"</note>

Pressing the heel well against the anus, draw up the air by force, again and again until it (the air) goes up. 62.

->When the upward breath, downward breath, internal sound, and the seed-point<-
->unite into one through the Root Lock, one undoubtedly attains success in Yoga. 63.<-
<note>original: "प्राणापानौ नादबिंदू मूलबंधेन चैकताम् ॥ गत्वा योगस्य संसिद्धिं गच्छतो नात्र संशयः ॥ ६३ ॥"</note>

<term>Prâna</term> <note>The upward-moving life force.</note>, <term>Apâna</term>, <term>Nâda</term> <note>The internal mystical sound heard during deep meditation.</note>, and <term>Bindu</term> <note>Literally "point" or "drop," representing the concentrated essence of creative energy or consciousness.</note> uniting into one in this way, give success in Yoga, undoubtedly. 63.

->By the union of the downward and upward breaths, urine and excrement decrease.<-
->Even an old man becomes young by constantly practicing the Root Lock. 64.<-
<note>original: "अपानप्राणयोरैक्यं क्षयो मूत्रपुरीषयोः ॥ युवा भवति वृद्धोऽपि सततं मूलबंधनात् ॥ ६४ ॥"</note>

By the purification <note>The original Sanskrit 'aikyam' implies union or oneness, though the commentary interprets this as a cleansing process.</note> of Prâna and Apâna, waste products decrease. Even an old man becomes young by constantly practicing Mûla Bandha. 64.

->When the downward breath moves upward and reaches the region of fire,<-
->then the flame of that fire grows long, fanned by the wind. 65.<-
<note>original: "अपाने ऊर्ध्वगे जाते प्रयाते वह्नि मंडलम् ॥ तदानलशिखा दीर्घा जायते वायुनाऽहता ॥ ६५ ॥"</note>

Going up, the Apâna enters the zone of fire, which is the stomach region. The flame of fire struck by the air is thereby lengthened. 65.

<margin>From the Sage Yajnavalkya:—</margin>
<note>original: "देहमध्ये शिखिस्थानं तप्तजाम्बूनदप्रभम् । त्रिकोणं तु मनुष्याणां चतुरस्रं चतुष्पदाम् ॥ मण्डलं तु पतंगानां सत्यमेतद्ब्रवीमि ते । तन्मध्येतु शिखातन्वी सदा तिष्ठति पावके ॥"</note>
In the center of the body is the seat of fire, glowing like heated gold. In humans, this region is triangular; in four-legged animals, it is square; and in birds, it is circular. I tell you the truth: in the midst of that fire, there always dwells a thin, delicate flame. This is the gastric fire <note>The internal heat responsible for digestion and spiritual transformation.</note>.

->Then, the fire and the downward breath reach the naturally hot upward breath.<-
->By this, the internal fire of the body becomes intensely inflamed. 66.<-
<note>original: "ततो यातो वह्न्यपानौ प्राणमुष्णस्वरूपकम् । तेनात्यंतप्रदीप्तस्तु ज्वलनो देहजस्तथा ॥ ६६ ॥"</note>

These, the fire and Apâna, go to the naturally hot Prâna, which, becoming inflamed thereby, causes a burning sensation in the body. 66.

->By this heat, the sleeping Kundalinî is thoroughly awakened.<-
->Like a serpent struck by a stick, she hisses and straightens herself. 67.<-
<note>original: "तेन कुण्डलिनी सुप्ता संतप्ता संप्रबुध्यते । दंडाता भुजंगीव निश्वस्य ऋजुतां व्रजेत् ॥ ६७ ॥"</note>

The <term>Kundalinî</term> <note>The primordial spiritual energy envisioned as a coiled serpent at the base of the spine.</note>, which has been sleeping all this time, becomes well-heated by this means and awakens fully. It becomes straight like a serpent struck by a stick. 67.

<summary>This page describes the technique and benefits of Mûla Bandha (the Root Lock), explaining how it redirects vital energies to fan the body's internal fire. This process is essential for awakening the Kundalinî energy, which is described as rising and straightening like a startled serpent.</summary>
<keywords>Mûla Bandha, Root Lock, Apâna, Prâna, Kundalinî, Gastric Fire, Yajnavalkya, Nâda, Bindu, Yoga physiology</keywords>
