# THE HATHA YOGA PRADIPIKA.

<meta>Institutional stamps are visible at the top left and right corners of the original document. Some minor foxing is present on the paper.</meta>

The sound which a <term>silent sage</term><note>original: "muni"; a practitioner of meditation, often one who observes a vow of silence.</note> hears by closing his ears with his fingers, should be heard attentively, till the mind becomes steady in it. 81.

->By practicing this <term>inner sound</term><note>original: "nâda"</note>, the external noises are eclipsed. By conquering all mental distractions within fifteen days, the <term>practitioner</term><note>original: "yogî"</note> becomes happy. 82.<-

By practicing with this <term>inner sound</term>, all other external sounds are stopped. The practitioner becomes happy by overcoming all distractions within 15 days. 82.

->In the first stage of practice, various loud sounds are heard. As the practice progresses, the sound heard becomes increasingly subtle. 83.<-

In the beginning, the sounds heard are of great variety and very loud; but, as the practice increases, they become more and more subtle. 83.

->Initially, the sounds resemble the ocean, thundering clouds, kettle-drums, and cymbals. In the middle stage, they arise like those from a double-headed drum, a conch, a bell, and a horn. 84.<-

In the first stage, the sounds are surging, thundering like the beating of kettle drums and jingling ones. In the intermediate stage, they are like those produced by a conch, a <term>double-headed drum</term><note>original: "Mridanga"</note>, bells, etc. 84.

->In the final stage, however, the sounds resemble tinkling bells, the flute, the <term>lute</term><note>original: "Vîṇâ"</note>, and the humming of a bee. Thus, these various kinds of sounds are heard as being produced within the body. 85.<-

In the last stage, the sounds resemble those from tinklets, flute, <term>lute</term>, bee, etc. These various kinds of sounds are heard as being produced in the body. 85.

->Even when loud sounds like those of thundering clouds and kettle-drums are being heard, one should focus specifically on the sound that is even more subtle. 86.<-

Though hearing loud sounds like those of thunder, kettle drums, etc., one should practice with the subtle sounds also. 86.

->Moving from the loud to the subtle, or from the subtle to the loud, the mind may find delight, but it should not be allowed to wander elsewhere. 87.<-

Leaving the loudest, taking up the subtle one, and leaving the subtle one, taking up the loudest, thus practicing, the distracted mind does not wander elsewhere. 87.

->Wherever the mind first attaches itself to a sound, it becomes firmly steady there, and eventually dissolves along with it. 88.<-

Wherever the mind attaches itself first, it becomes steady there; and then it becomes absorbed in it. 88.

->Just as a bee drinking nectar does not notice the fragrance, the mind absorbed in the <term>inner sound</term> does not crave the objects of the senses. 89.<-

<note>The text concludes mid-sentence at the bottom of the page, continuing the analogy of the bee and the nectar.</note>

<summary>This page details the progression of inner sounds (Nada) heard during meditation, ranging from loud percussive noises to subtle melodic tones, and explains how focusing on these sounds leads to mental absorption.</summary>
<keywords>Nada Yoga, meditation, inner sound, sensory withdrawal, mental absorption, Hatha Yoga Pradipika, Vina, Mridanga</keywords>

--- Page 68 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical instructions for Nada Yoga (the Yoga of Inner Sound). While the previous page focused on the stages of practice and the variety of sounds heard—ranging from thunder to the delicate chime of a flute—this page describes how these sounds act as tools for mental control and ultimate liberation.</meta>

# THE HATHA YOGA PRADIPIKA.

<meta>Institutional stamps are visible at the top left and right corners of the original document.</meta>

Just as a bee, drinking sweet juice, does not care for the smell of the flower; so the mind, absorbed in the <term>inner sound</term><note>original: "nâda"; the mystical internal vibration heard during deep meditation.</note>, no longer desires the objects of worldly enjoyment. 89.

->The mind is like a great rutting elephant wandering in the garden of the senses; it is capable of being controlled by the sharp goad of the <term>unstruck sound</term><note>original: "anâhata nâda"; the internal sound that arises without any physical contact or "striking."</note>. 90.<-

->The mind, captured in the snare of the inner sound, casts off its restlessness; and, like a bird with clipped wings, it becomes perfectly still at once. 91.<-

->Those who desire the kingdom of <term>union</term><note>original: "Yoga"</note> should practice listening to the inner sound with a concentrated mind, free from all other thoughts and cares. 92.<-

->The inner sound is the snare for catching the mind; and, when the mind is caught like a deer, it can be stilled<note>original: "vadha" (literally: killed/slain); in the context of Yoga, this refers to "manolaya" or the dissolution of the individual ego-mind into pure consciousness.</note> just as easily. 93.<-

->The inner sound acts like the bolt on a stable door for the horse of the mind. A <term>practitioner</term><note>original: "yogî"</note> should firmly resolve to practice the hearing of these sounds constantly. 94.<-

->The mind attains the properties of <term>processed mercury</term><note>original: "pârada"; a metaphor from Indian alchemy (Rasayana). Just as volatile mercury is "killed" or solidified through chemical processes to become medicine, the wandering mind is solidified or made steady when combined with the "sulphur" of the inner sound.</note>. When deprived of its unsteadiness, the mind is "fixed" through the inner sound and then roams in the supportless <term>pure space</term><note>original: "âkâśa" or "Brahma"; refers here to the state of void or pure consciousness where the mind no longer relies on external objects.</note>. 95.<-

->The mind is like a serpent; hearing the inner sound, it forgets its unsteadiness and does not wander away anywhere. 96.<-

<summary>This page uses vivid animal and alchemical metaphors to describe how the "nâda" (inner sound) acts as a powerful tether for the wandering mind, leading to a state of complete stillness.</summary>
<keywords>Nada Yoga, mental control, anahata nada, Indian alchemy, Pârada, stillness, meditation, Hatha Yoga Pradipika</keywords>

--- Page 69 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical instructions for Nada Yoga (the Yoga of Inner Sound). While the previous page focused on the stages of practice and the variety of sounds heard—ranging from thunder to the delicate chime of a flute—this page describes how these sounds act as tools for mental control and ultimate liberation. Institutional stamps are visible at the top left and right corners of the original document.</meta>

# THE HATHA YOGA PRADIPIKA.

->A fire that is started in a piece of wood is extinguished along with the wood itself once it has been consumed; in the same way, the mind, when focused on the <term>inner sound</term><note>original: "nâda"; the internal mystical vibration.</note>, becomes quiet and still along with the sound. 97.<-

->The <term>inner mind</term><note>original: "antahkaraṇa"</note> is like a deer that becomes captivated and motionless upon hearing the ringing of bells and other sounds; it is then very easy for an expert archer to strike it down. 98.<-
<note>In this metaphor, the yogi is the archer, and the "killing" of the deer represents the dissolution of the restless ego-mind into a state of pure consciousness.</note>

->The knowable truth resides within the <term>unstruck sound</term><note>original: "anâhata"; the sound heard in meditation that is produced without physical contact.</note>, and the mind resides within that knowable truth. When the mind is absorbed there, it reaches the highest state of the all-pervading Lord. 99.<-

->As long as the sounds continue, the concept of <term>spiritual space</term><note>original: "âkâśa"</note> remains. When the sounds cease, that state is called the <term>Supreme Reality</term><note>original: "Para Brahma"</note> or the <term>Supreme Self</term><note>original: "Paramâtmana"</note>. 100.<-

->Whatever is heard in the form of the inner sound is actually the manifestation of <term>divine energy</term><note>original: "śakti"</note>. That which is formless and the ultimate end of all <term>essential principles</term><note>original: "tatwas"</note> is the Supreme Lord himself. 101.<-

->All the physical methods of <term>Forceful Yoga</term><note>original: "Haṭha"</note> are practiced to achieve success in <term>Royal Yoga</term><note>original: "Râja-Yoga"</note>; the person who is established in Royal Yoga overcomes time and death. 102.<-

->Truth is the seed, Hatha Yoga is the field, and <term>dispassion</term><note>original: "vairâgya"; a state of indifference toward worldly desires</note> is the water. Through the combination of these three, the <term>state of no-mind</term><note>original: "unmanî"</note>—likened here to a wish-fulfilling vine—blooms immediately. 103.<-

->By constantly focusing on the inner sound, the accumulation of past sins is destroyed. The mind and the breath are surely dissolved into the <term>Stainless One</term><note>original: "Niranjana"; a title for the pure, unconditioned consciousness.</note>. 104.<-

<summary>This page explains the dissolution of the mind through Nada Yoga, using metaphors like fire consuming wood and a deer caught by sound to describe how the practitioner reaches the "Supreme State" (Raja Yoga). It emphasizes that all physical Hatha Yoga practices are ultimately intended to lead to this mental absorption and liberation from death.</summary>

<keywords>Nada Yoga, inner sound, Anahata, Raja Yoga, mental dissolution, Unmani, liberation, Hatha Yoga Pradipika</keywords>

--- Page 70 ---
<meta>This page concludes the technical section on Nada Yoga (the Yoga of Inner Sound) and details the characteristics of a practitioner who has successfully entered Samadhi, the state of total meditative absorption. The text continues from the previous discussion on how the mind dissolves into the internal vibrations.</meta>

# THE HATHA YOGA PRADIPIKA.

All accumulated impurities are destroyed by constant practice with the <term>inner sound</term><note>original: "nâda"; the internal mystical vibration.</note>; the mind and the vital breaths then certainly dissolve into the <term>Supreme Self</term><note>original: "Paramâtmana"; the absolute, unconditioned reality often described as being beyond qualities or "colorless."</note>. 104.

->He never hears the blast of the conch or the boom of the <term>kettle-drum</term><note>original: "Dundubhi"</note>.<-
->In this <term>state beyond the mind</term><note>original: "Unmanî avasthâ"</note>, his body surely becomes as still as a piece of wood. 105.<-

->Released from all mental states and free from every worry,<-
->the <term>practitioner</term><note>original: "Yogî"</note> remains as if dead; he is liberated, of this there is no doubt. 106.<-

->He is not consumed by death, nor is he bound by the fruits of his actions.<-
->The practitioner established in <term>deep absorption</term><note>original: "Samâdhi"</note> cannot be conquered by anyone. 107.<-

->He perceives neither smell, taste, form, touch, nor sound;<-
->the practitioner in deep absorption is conscious neither of himself nor of others. 108.<-

->When the mind is neither sleeping nor waking, beyond both remembering and forgetting,<-
->neither setting like the sun nor rising, such a person is truly liberated. 109.<-

->He recognizes neither heat nor cold, neither pain nor pleasure,<-
->nor honor nor dishonor. Such a practitioner is absorbed in the deep state of meditation. 110.<-

->He who remains stable as if in deep sleep while actually awake,<-
->and whose breath has ceased its coming and going, is certainly liberated. 111.<-

->He cannot be harmed by any weapon, nor overcome by any living being;<-
->the practitioner in deep absorption is beyond the reach of <term>sacred chants</term><note>original: "mantras"</note> or <term>mystical diagrams</term><note>original: "yantras"</note>. 112.<-

<summary>This page describes the final state of Samadhi, where the practitioner transcends the physical senses, the flow of time, and the laws of karma, becoming effectively liberated from the material world.</summary>
<keywords>Samadhi, liberation, inner sound, transcendence, mental stillness, Hatha Yoga, immortality</keywords>

--- Page 71 ---
<meta>This page serves as the conclusion to the fourth and final chapter of the Hatha Yoga Pradipika. It follows the detailed instructions on Samadhi (deep meditative absorption) and the practice of Nada Yoga (meditating on inner sounds). The text here provides the final criteria for success in Hatha Yoga, asserting that internal energetic mastery must precede claims of spiritual enlightenment.</meta>

# THE HATHA YOGA PRADIPIKA.

->As long as the <term>vital breath</term><note>original: "Prāṇa"</note> does not enter and flow into the <term>central channel</term><note>original: "madhyamārge"; the Sushumna, the primary spiritual passage along the spine.</note>,<-
->and as long as the <term>vital essence</term><note>original: "vindu"</note> does not become stable through the firm control of the movements of the breath;<-
->as long as the mind does not effortlessly assume the form of the <term>Absolute Reality</term><note>original: "Brahma" or "tattva"</note> during meditation;<-
->until then, all talk of spiritual knowledge and wisdom is merely the hypocritical and false babbling of a madman. 113.<-

->Thus ends the fourth chapter, titled "The Characteristics of <term>Deep Absorption</term><note>original: "Samādhi-lakṣaṇam"</note>," in the *Hatha Yoga Pradipika*. 4.<-

***

->THE END.<-

<summary>This concluding page of the Hatha Yoga Pradipika emphasizes that true spiritual knowledge is only attained when the breath enters the central channel and the mind merges with the Absolute; without these physical and energetic signs, claims of enlightenment are dismissed as false. It marks the end of the fourth chapter and the entire treatise.</summary>

<keywords>Prana, middle channel, Bindu, Brahma, Samadhi, spiritual realization, Hatha Yoga Pradipika conclusion</keywords>
